- **[ARCHER: From Rationale Imitation to Executable Reasoning in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18494)**  `arXiv:2611.18494`  
  _Dhruv Dixit, Khushi Patel, Congcong Xu, Min Song_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small language models are attractive for local, low-cost, and privacy-sensitive deployment, but they remain unreliable on mathematical reasoning tasks that require decomposition, exact computation, tool use, and verification. We introduce \textbf{ARCHER}: \textbf{A}ction-trace \textbf{R}einforcement for \textbf{C}alculator-grounded \textbf{H}ierarchical \textbf{E}xecutable \textbf{R}easoning, a two-stage framework for training small language models (SLMs) to produce executable and auditable reasoning trajectories. In the first stage, ARCHER compiles free-form chain-of-thought rationales into typed action traces containing reasoning steps, calculator calls, deterministic runtime observations, verification actions, and final answers. This shifts the supervision target from imitating explanatory text to learning a tool-grounded reasoning policy. In the second stage, ARCHER refines this policy through asymmetric hierarchical reinforcement learning, using grouped and conditionally gated trajectory rewards for answer correctness, tool grounding, process discipline, and trace alignment. Across five standard sub-10B backbones, ARCHER improves over the strongest tool-augmented baseline by 5.51 percentage points on macro-average across benchmark accuracies, including gains of 8.95 points on MATH-500 and 5.33 and 5.34 points on AIME-2024 and AIME-2025. Evaluations on two additional reasoning-specialized 7B backbones further show that executable grounding remains beneficial after reasoning-focused reinforcement learning or distillation. These results demonstrate that executable supervision and reward-guided behavioral refinement provide a general and effective training paradigm for compact mathematical reasoners.
  </details>

- **[Typing Matters: Muscle, Language and Typing Behaviour in Live Keystroke Decoding from sEMG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18464)**  `arXiv:2611.18464`  
  _Abhilash Durgam, Nyle Siddiqui, Mubarak Shah, Qiushi Fu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surface electromyography (sEMG) from the wrists can recover typed text without a keyboard. It is usually posed as a signal-inference problem: characters are read out of the muscle signal and character error rate is reported offline. We argue that this framing removes most of the problem. The decoder is constrained simultaneously by the muscles, by the language being typed, and by typing behaviour such as typos, corrections, backspaces and line breaks, and a usable system must predict keys live while the hand keeps moving. Existing systems fall short on two counts: (1) they process sEMG in fixed windows and carry nothing from one window to the next, discarding context accumulated over a session, and (2) they lack any understanding of the linguistic structure that drives typing, so every keystroke is inferred from the muscle signal alone. We propose a fully causal, zero-lookahead sEMG typing decoder built on a state space model (SSM), in which a single model decodes a whole session without windowed partitions, conditioned on what has already been typed. We achieve this by fusing a traditional typing decoder with an efficient autoregressive language model, instilling both sEMG and linguistic understanding in one model. To train such a model for per-character typing rather than whole-word text, we introduce a retokenization method that rewrites ordinary prose as human-like typing, with keystrokes, typos, backspaces and line breaks included, converting WikiText into a large typing corpus where none existed. Our model, having never seen a single sEMG-to-character transcript in typing, achieves a state-of-the-art character error rate of 25.13% on emg2qwerty's cross-user benchmark, a 5.4% relative improvement over the previous best with 6.7 times fewer parameters, while running strictly causally with no future context.
  </details>

- **[When to Trust Simulated Patient Responses:Support-Gated World Models for Vasopressor Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18458)**  `arXiv:2611.18458`  
  _Yifan Jin, Tao Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical world models expose language-model agents to predicted patient responses, but simulations can be unreliable for poorly supported treatments or unfamiliar hospitals. We introduce Support-Gated World-Model decision support, a lightweight extension of a propose--simulate--refine agent. A recurrent dynamics ensemble predicts short-horizon physiological responses to documented norepinephrine dose categories, while a behavior model estimates observational action support. The interface withholds predictions with insufficient support or excessive ensemble disagreement and abstains when no candidate qualifies. Evaluation on eICU-CRD includes 70 hospitals and 30,700 decision transitions, with 14 hospitals held out for testing. At 62.0% factual-transition coverage, joint gating yields a MAP mean absolute error of 7.3 mmHg, compared with 9.2 mmHg for random selection at the same coverage. The gated agent achieves 60.0% agreement with recorded actions at 81.0% decision coverage, compared with 55.0% agreement and 99.5% coverage for an ungated ensemble agent. Component ablations show lower factual risk with joint gating than with either gatethis http URLresults characterize a reliability--coverage trade-off in selective clinical simulation; they do not identify individualized treatment effects or establish improved patient outcomes.
  </details>

- **[LECAT: Measuring LLM Category Learning from Pretraining Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18419)**  `arXiv:2611.18419`  
  _Jimin Mun, Maarten Sap, Yanai Elazar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans know that airplanes are fast, that this extends to red airplanes, but that it does not hold for broken airplanes. How do LLMs acquire category knowledge that humans learn and apply effortlessly? We introduce LeCat, a two-part framework to analyze LLM's Learning of Category knowledge. First, we use LeCat to evaluate models on their ability to learn category knowledge, distinguishing between three cases: memorization of in-domain category knowledge, generalization to unseen instances, and ability to flexibly use knowledge in appropriate contexts. Then, we use LeCat to trace how this knowledge can be learned in pretraining from text that may encode category knowledge in various ways, such as verbatim repetition or exceptions, which we refer to as forms of "knowledge expression". We first assess open-source pretrained models using 199 curated category-feature pairs and analyze their training data. Our findings suggest that strengthening context and category-feature co-occurrence likelihood (PMI) improve learning for larger models and verbatim rule-like statements diminish flexible category learning. To systematically isolate these effects, we then train a 68M model from scratch with precise control over the categorical information each sees in their training data, confirming that verbatim repetition reduces flexible learning but mixture of diverse knowledge expressions lead to more robust category learning. Together, our findings highlight that while contextual flexibility is persistently difficult for models, how category knowledge is expressed in pretraining greatly impacts models' acquisition of robust category knowledge.
  </details>

- **[VelServe: Taming Decode Jitters in LLM Inference with Tail-Calibrated Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18392)**  `arXiv:2611.18392`  
  _Ishaan Datta, Alind Khare, Koushik Kumar Dey, Ratnesh Jamidar, Anand Kumar Singh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive LLM services must meet latency service-level objectives (SLOs) for the time to first token (TTFT) and the time between tokens (TBT), as streaming and soft real-time applications require predictable token delivery. We identify decode jitters in both aggregated and disaggregated serving. A jitter delays every request in the batch, and long responses span hundreds of steps, so even rare overruns cause many SLO violations. In aggregated serving, SLO-aware schedulers set each step's prefill budget until its predicted latency just meets the TBT target, leaving no room for mispredictions or host stalls. Disaggregation meets average TBT targets at a cost in capacity, yet the steps that admit new requests still run late.We present \sysname, a scheduler that calibrates how much prefill work each decode step carries using the measured tail of its own iteration latency. The SLO determines how rare a late step must be, and \sysname leaves enough headroom in each iteration to prevent overruns and meet the target decode latency. This headroom slows prefill, so \sysname serves the shortest waiting prompt first. When TTFT deadlines grow with prompt length, the long prompts it delays have the most time to spare. Under disaggregation, it spaces admissions so that no request waits on more slow admission steps than its P99 target allows. On three attention families and varying workloads, \sysname sustains 2.4--2.9$\times$ the SLO goodput of the strongest baseline on Qwen3-8B and DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and at least 4.4$\times$ on DeepSeek-V2-Lite. With prefill and decode disaggregated on eight GPUs, \sysname meets the SLO for more than twice as many requests as state-of-the-art baselines.
  </details>

- **[VLAN: Vision-Language Accessible Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18346)**  `arXiv:2611.18346`  
  _Jie Hu, Yu Zheng, Wenzhi Wu, Weixing Feng, Cunlei Cheng, Jiaming Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accessible mobility is user-conditioned rather than scene-intrinsic, as walkable paths for one embodiment are inaccessible to another. Yet, existing benchmarks evaluate traversability under a fixed and limited embodiment. We present Vision-Language Accessible Navigation (VLAN), containing 1,000 scenes, 20K navigation trajectories, and 160K keyframes across diverse lighting, environmental conditions, scene semantics, and path geometries. For each trajectory, VLAN provides embodiment-specific actions and accessibility features for legged and wheeled agents, including humanoid and quadruped robots and people with visual or mobility impairments, while everyday obstacles and accessibility labels are categorized as Passable, Bypass, HighRisk, and Blocked. Unlike goal-only evaluation, our benchmark measures physical safety and alignment with user-specific constraints via goal-reaching, collision-aware, and accessibility-aligned metrics. We benchmark modern vision-language models and find they often fail to consistently adapt obstacle relevance, path selection, and safety criteria across user profiles, suggesting accessibility-aware navigation requires dedicated datasets, tasks, and metrics beyond conventional embodied navigation evaluation. All data and code open-source atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Causal AI-Driven Secure mmWave Beam Alignment with Defensive Variational Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18321)**  `arXiv:2611.18321`  
  _Nasir Khan, Asmaa Abdallah, Abdulkadir Celik, Ahmed M. Eltawil, Sinem Coleri, Cicek Cavdar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient and secure beam alignment is essential for mmWave communication systems. However, existing deep learning (DL)-based beam alignment methods often neglect causal relationships between inputs and outputs, resulting in limited interpretability, poor generalization, and excessive beam sweeping. Moreover, these DL solutions are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, where small perturbations in received power measurements can mislead beam classifiers. To overcome these issues, we propose a causally-aware DL framework integrating causal discovery into beam alignment. A two-stage causal beam selection algorithm learns a Bayesian graph capturing dependencies between received signal strength indicators (RSSI) as inputs and the optimal beam index as the target variable, then uses it to select a minimal set of relevant input features for the DL classifier. To mitigate adversarial attacks, we introduce a defensive variational autoencoder (DVAE) that maps potentially perturbed inputs back to their clean versions. End-to-end fine-tuning of the downstream beam classifier on the DVAE-reconstructed RSSI representations further boosts prediction accuracy. Simulation results show that causal beam selection achieves accuracy comparable to conventional methods while providing an \(\mathbf{829\times}\) lower per-sample computational cost than SHAP (Shapley Additive Explanations), a widely used explainable AI (XAI)-based feature selection method. It further reduces beam-sweeping overhead by \(\mathbf{59.4\%}\) and \(\mathbf{89.8\%}\), compared to conventional DL-based beam selection and exhaustive search, respectively. The DVAE-based defense effectively denoises adversarial inputs, sustaining 85\% accuracy across different perturbation bounds, retains baseline performance on unperturbed RSSI inputs and surpasses defensive distillation defense scheme by 15--20\% on average under different attack scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Experts Across Schemas: Agent-Guided Mixture-of-Relational-Experts for Cross-Database Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18315)**  `arXiv:2611.18315`  
  _Hiren Madhu, Sukanta Ganguly, Kiran Srinivasan, Smita Krishnaswamy, Rex Ying_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web platforms record user and item activity in relational databases whose schemas differ from platform to platform, which makes it difficult to transfer predictive models between them. Relational foundation models address this through cross-database pretraining, yet recent improvements have relied on increasingly large pretraining corpora. We ask whether a better architecture, together with features specific to the target database, can deliver comparable gains from far less pretraining data. We introduce MoREGT, a mixture-of-relational-experts graph transformer that represents both rows and cells as tokens, adds the log-ages of rows and the names of foreign-key relationships to attention, and routes cells and rows to experts by column metadata rather than by values. A label-blind LLM agent complements the model: it reads the target database's schema and column statistics and adds validated single-row derived columns, without access to labels or model scores. On 21 RelBench tasks with leave-one-database-out pretraining on six databases, the MoREGT backbone improves on the Relational Transformer pretrained on the same databases by 2.25 points of mean AUROC and 5.83 points of mean nMAE. The agent's columns raise mean AUROC from 78.45 to 79.63 and lower mean nMAE from 27.91 to 26.98, the highest means on both metrics among the compared methods, including RT-J, which pretrains on about 400 times as many databases.
  </details>

- **[Probabilities of Causation in Nondeterministic Causal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18245)**  `arXiv:2611.18245`  
  _David S. Watson, Sander Beckers_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study probabilities of causation in nondeterministic causal models, where mechanisms may remain intrinsically stochastic even after all latent variables are fixed. Focusing on binary treatments and outcomes, we develop a generic identification program that represents observational and interventional data as moment constraints on latent stochastic mechanism types and computes minimal/maximal values of counterfactual quantities via constrained optimization. Within this framework, we derive sharp analytic bounds on the probabilities of causation under several assumptions tailored to nondeterministic models. Resulting identification regions are often more informative than those attainable under deterministic alternatives. We illustrate the practical relevance of these results in a case study on personalized treatment decisions and experiments on semi-synthetic datasets. Our results clarify how classical bounds relate to nondeterministic causal semantics, generalizing previous works and providing a unified route from qualitative structural restrictions to computable partial identification regions.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Model-Empowered Intelligent Agent for Adaptive Fast-Charging Control of Lithium-Ion Batteries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18140)**  `arXiv:2611.18140`  
  _Joey Chan, Haoyu Pan, Zhen Chen, ErShun Pan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fast charging of lithium-ion batteries is a multi-objective control problem that requires reducing charging time and sustaining high energy throughput while suppressing accelerated degradation and safety-critical electrochemical reactions. Conventional protocols are easy to implement but have limited ability to adapt to variations in battery chemistry, operating conditions, and aging state. Model-based optimization and conventional data-driven control often require accurate models, costly iterative search, or task-specific policies. We develop a large language model (LLM)-empowered intelligent agent for adaptive battery fast-charging control. In this closed-loop framework, the agent interprets structured battery states, generates executable charging actions, observes the resulting battery response, and continuously adjusts subsequent decisions. We construct a large-scale simulation corpus with PyBaMM across five battery chemistries and 11 parameter sets and convert the resulting state-action-response trajectories into structured process data for agent training. Our two-stage strategy combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). SFT establishes battery-control semantics and feasible decision priors from simulation-derived demonstrations. GRPO aligns the agent with a multi-objective control objective covering energy throughput, degradation mitigation, and operational constraints. Closed-loop simulations show that, under matched energy-throughput requirements, the agent achieves longer cycling life than conventional charging baselines and optimization-based strategies across heterogeneous battery configurations. This study provides a simulation-grounded proof of concept for using LLM agents as sequential decision makers in battery management and takes a step toward language-model-enabled control of physical energy systems.
  </details>

- **[JFinBench: A Continuously Refreshable Japanese Financial Benchmark from the Latest Certification Exams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18130)**  `arXiv:2611.18130`  
  _Issa Sugiura, Taro Makino, Takashi Ishida_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to finance, creating a need for benchmarks that evaluate their capabilities across financial systems with different regulations and practices. However, existing financial benchmarks provide limited coverage of country-specific financial systems, and they are difficult to keep up to date as released questions leak into training data. We introduce JFinBench, a Japanese financial benchmark comprising 321 examples from the 2026 editions of six regularly administered certification exams covering accounting, tax, financial planning, and banking. To construct it, we develop a human-in-the-loop agentic pipeline in which a coding agent writes exam-specific conversion code and a review page, while a human verifies every example and feeds corrections back to the agent. This pipeline adapts to heterogeneous exam formats and reduces the effort of incorporating new exam editions, enabling periodic refreshes that mitigate data contamination. Evaluating 12 recent LLMs, we find that Claude Fable 5 and GPT-6 Astra exceed 93% overall, while even the best open-weight model, Kimi K3.1 Pro, trails Claude Fable 5 by 12 percentage points. When models can additionally use a terminal and web access, they retrieve the knowledge they lack and every model improves. The gap narrows but about 4 points remain, and the ranking of models stays largely the same, suggesting that knowledge held in the model itself remains valuable even with tools. We release the dataset, the evaluation code, and the construction pipeline.
  </details>

- **[Do Machines Learn Deviance From Their Peers? A Randomized Test of Differential Association With AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18098)**  `arXiv:2611.18098`  
  _Murat Ozer, Arif Akgul, Amitabh Chakravorty_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Differential association theory and its extension, social learning theory, hold that rule-breaking, and the definitions that make it acceptable, are learned through interaction with others. Because people choose their friends, this claim has been hard to test. We addressed this selection problem with AI agents, which learn only through communication and can be assigned to peers at random. In a preregistered experiment, 420 teams of three agents (1,260 agents; seven AI models) worked on coding tasks that could not be solved honestly. A scripted teammate said that it had cheated (deviant peer), that it had reported the problem honestly (honest peer), or said nothing (silent peer). Half of the teams were told that an auditor read their chat. Compared with a silent peer, a deviant peer increased cheating (66.2% vs. 62.9%; OR = 1.96) and an honest peer reduced it (55.7%; OR = 0.35). Among agents that cheated, a deviant peer increased claims that cheating was normal (13.7% vs. 3.4%), often as the team's norm. Monitoring did not suppress prohibited discussion; it doubled it. Peers mattered only for models that had not yet decided. AI agents offer a new way to test criminological learning theories without selection effects.
  </details>

- **[Two-Clustering Regime of Token Dynamics in Causal Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18088)**  `arXiv:2611.18088`  
  _Trinh T. Nguyen, Duy-Tung Pham, Hoang-Son Do, Tan Minh Nguyen, Thieu N. Vo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We partially resolve Conjecture 1 of [Karagodin et al., Clustering in Causal Attention Masking (NeurIPS 2024)] and extend it to time-dependent parameter regimes. The conjecture predicts that causal attention dynamics converge to two clusters aligned with the eigenvectors associated with the largest eigenvalue $\lambda_{\max}$ of the value transformation. We prove that this behavior holds when $\lambda_{\max}>0$ and is generally false when $\lambda_{\max}\le0$. The resulting two-cluster regime is common in practice and substantially more challenging than the classical single-cluster setting studied previously. A central contribution of our work is the identification of a rigorous dimension reduction phenomenon: despite evolving in a high-dimensional embedding space, token trajectories collapse onto a two-dimensional structure governed by a small number of dominant directions. Building on this insight, we introduce a new analytical framework--instability within the flow--inspired by instability phenomena in physical systems. This framework enables the analysis of steady-regime behavior in non-monotone, non-autonomous attention dynamics. Our theoretical findings are illustrated through simulations and experimental studies using Group Query Attention (GQA) layers.
  </details>

- **[A$^3$: Adaptive Audio Agent Framework for Collaborative Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18064)**  `arXiv:2611.18064`  
  _Zexin Li, Xueyi Zhang, Tao Ren, Yiming Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embedded audio agents need a nearby edge server for speed, but a server-dependent agent returns nothing when the link fails. We present A$^3$, an adaptive audio agent framework that places whole pipeline stages on the server, keeps a complete fallback agent on devices that can host one, and hedges both paths with a link-aware deadline that decides when to give up on the server. On Jetson devices and an RTX A4500 server, A$^3$ matches the server-only agent on a healthy link, answers every request in a full outage on the AGX, and beats a percentile hedge in simulated outages.
  </details>

- **[Understanding the limits of snapshot-based fault diagnosis in distribution networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17998)**  `arXiv:2611.17998`  
  _Ba Tu Phung, Huy Hoang Le, Kim-Anh Nguyen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inverter-based resources weaken fault currents in distribution networks, and snapshot-based diagnosis, which classifies a fault from voltage phasors at a few buses at one instant, offers a cheap first-stage screen. Its published accuracies, however, rest on different noise assumptions and do not show whether the error comes from the classifier, the features, the measurement error, or the fault resistance. This paper separates these sources on three IEEE test feeders and a public data set with distributed generation, using four feature sets, thirteen classifiers, and measurement error defined by total vector error classes. The classifier is not the limiting factor, because the full phasor snapshot classifies every fault correctly without noise, and the remaining error comes from measurement error on high-impedance faults. Three voltage sensors classify 90-96% of faults at the 1% accuracy class and 96-99% with distribution micro phasor measurement units (micro-PMUs). A discriminability-margin placement selects them from simulations alone in under one second and matches the best of twelve placement methods. The results show when snapshot-based diagnosis can be trusted.
  </details>

- **[LACCO: Local Accumulation with Computation--Communication Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17991)**  `arXiv:2611.17991`  
  _Daniele Kopyshevskiy, Benjamin Th{é}rien, Nicolas Pinto, Edouard Oyallon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training language models across low-bandwidth networks requires reducing synchronization overhead. Local optimization methods such as DiLoCo communicate only after several local steps, but outer gradient averaging and the outer update remain on the critical path. Existing computation-communication overlap methods avoid this stall through delayed or replica-local updates, which can degrade convergence or cause replicas to drift. We propose LACCO (Local Accumulation with Computation-Communication Overlap), a two-stage algorithm that overlaps global communication with local computation while applying globally aggregated updates shared by every replica, without introducing staleness in the outer update. We establish convergence guarantees for smooth non-convex objectives. Experiments on language-model pre-training with FineWeb show that LACCO consistently outperforms communication-overlapped baselines such as Eager and Delayed DiLoCo. These gains remain robust as model size scales up to 0.5B parameters and across a range of communication intervals and numbers of replicas up to 16 workers, while incurring only negligible-or fully overlappable-computational or memory overhead, which demonstrates the strength of LACCO.
  </details>

- **[Interactive deep-learning based interface for brain tumour segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17983)**  `arXiv:2611.17983`  
  _Enora Giffard, Pierre Jannin, John S H Baxter_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Brain tumour segmentation from MR images is an integral part of their treatment, either through surgical resection or radiotherapy. Both applications require a high degree of precision that can be difficult to ensure for all patients due to the high degree of variability in the size, shape, number, and location of these tumours. This leads to difficulties with their automatic segmentation via deep learning, which can display catastrophic segmentation errors when presented with images that differ, even subtly, in terms of the specific MR sequence used, which varies from vendor to vendor. This article presents a deep-learning-based interactive segmentation system that, while being fully automatic by default, has the capability of being easily corrected by human users, allowing them ensure sufficiently high brain tumour segmentation accuracy for all patients. This interface is evaluated both in terms of the accuracy of its automatic approach as well as its usability through a preliminary observational expert user study.
  </details>

- **[TotalSegmentator Aorta Report: Automated Aortic Diameter Measurement at 11 Standardized Landmarks on CT Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17968)**  `arXiv:2611.17968`  
  _Jakob Wasserthal, Daniel Hinck, Martin Segeroth, Joshy Cyriac, Denis Berdajs, Jules Miazza, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: To develop and evaluate a fully automated, open-source tool that measures aortic diameters at 11 standardized landmarks from the aortic annulus to the bifurcation on any CT image, following current guideline recommendations.Materials and Methods: This retrospective single-center study included 239 arterial phase CT angiography examinations of 230 patients (median age, 65 years [range, 18-99 years]; 163 men) performed between April 2025 and October 2026 (209 electrocardiographically [ECG] gated). The tool locates landmarks from TotalSegmentator segmentations of aortic branches and vertebrae and measures the maximum diameter at each. A senior radiologist reviewed and, where needed, corrected the automated measurements to create the reference standard. Agreement was assessed with mean absolute error (MAE), bias, and differences greater than 2 mm.Results: Across all landmarks, MAE was 1.06 mm (95% CI: 0.91, 1.22), and 86.6% (95% CI: 84.4, 88.5) of differences were 2 mm or less. The tool measured slightly larger diameters (bias, 0.45 mm [95% CI: 0.34, 0.57]). The sinuses of Valsalva had the largest MAE (2.17 mm [95% CI: 1.59, 2.86]). For 1.2% of landmarks, the automatic measurement failed. Examination-level MAE did not differ significantly by pathology, atherosclerosis grade (P=.95), or ECG gating (P=.14). Examinations with mural thrombus more often had an MAE greater than 2 mm (50% [95% CI: 31, 69] vs 20%; P=.03).Conclusion: The tool measured aortic diameters at 11 landmarks along the entire aorta with an MAE of about 1 mm, below published interreader variability. Agreement was lower at the aortic root and with mural thrombus.Code availability: The tool is open source and freely available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OutfitterBench: Evaluating LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Resource Allocation and Commitment Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17933)**  `arXiv:2611.17933`  
  _Jie Huang, Yuecheng Hong, Yao Chen, Shengding Jiao, Xudong Liu, Qiang Ye_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In many long-horizon settings, an agent's own choices create future constraints: committing finite capacity establishes future obligations and forecloses later opportunities. Sustaining performance therefore requires both keeping evolving plans feasible and comparing the value of resource allocations with the alternatives they displace. Recent benchmarks increasingly evaluate LLM agents in continuing operations, but give less attention to how agents keep their own commitments feasible while reassessing resource allocations as opportunities change. We introduce OutfitterBench, where agents manage an outdoor-equipment rental business for 200 days, purchasing from a 46-product catalog and handling roughly 1,100 reservation requests. Under a fixed capital ceiling, reservations and uncertain walk-in demand compete for reusable equipment whose availability changes over time. Experiments with seven frontier LLMs show that several agents underperform a simple rule-based baseline, while only the leading agent consistently surpasses it. Trajectory analysis shows that some agents apply known constraints inconsistently and make new decisions that conflict with existing commitments. Agents with more reliable fulfillment can still secure low returns from comparable commitments or delay resource reallocation. The leading agent better connects preparation with economic choices but also misses opportunities for more profitable asset use. These findings highlight limitations in sustaining coherent resource plans and reassessing their value over time. The benchmark code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Large Language Models for Scientific Paper Search: Retrieval Success and Inference Latency on Mac Studio and NVIDIA A100](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17929)**  `arXiv:2611.17929`  
  _Frederic Andriantseheno Rakoto, Yannis Bendi-Ouis, Xavier Hinaut_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific literature search can benefit from locally deployed LLM assistants, which reduce reliance on hosted model providers while giving research teams greater control over their data and infrastructure. This work investigates whether open-weight models can support web-assisted paper retrieval under practical computing constraints. We first characterize inference latency as the input context grows from 512 to 128k tokens on a Mac Studio and a server equipped with two NVIDIA A100 GPUs. We then evaluate eight models on the retrieval of 70 papers from seven scientific repositories, using queries that represent three levels of domain knowledge. The results show that retrieval success depends strongly on the selected model and the specificity of the query. And that A100 GPUs are generally faster than the Mac Studio, but its unified memory allows larger models that could not be loaded in the tested GPU configuration.
  </details>

- **[What Balanced Accuracy Cannot Identify in Neural-Signal Decoding under Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17925)**  `arXiv:2611.17925`  
  _Jiangshuo Xie, Zonghan Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adaptation for neural-signal classification is usually transductive: each batch is scored after updating on it, and the gain depends on the adapted model [4]. This paper asks what an aggregate change fails to characterise inside the cohort. Full test-time adaptation (full_ttt) was evaluated in 11 dataset-encoder cells (four each on Siena/EEG and PTB-XL/ECG, three on Sleep-EDF/EEG), with splits and source-model evaluation fixed; the subject was the unit, exact sign tests were Holm-corrected and conditional on the shared stream, and bootstrap intervals accompanied a dependence audit. Two results of different kinds follow. In the binary case balanced accuracy gives a one-class decoder the same value as one reporting both classes at 50%, so the aggregate cannot identify a class that stopped being reported; a class covering a tenth of a subject's samples went unpredicted in 26-47 of 112 subject-cells, against 7-9 for the controls, which is a descriptive count. With cohort, split and update rule fixed, the within-subject class contrast follows the encoder, tested directly rather than inferred from tests against zero: two of six Siena encoder pairs survive the dependence readings at their nominal reference and one at a fold-level reference, while two of ten Sleep-EDF stage pairs pass the run-clustered gate at its nominal reference; aggregate and contrast named different encoders in 10 of 11. The protocol bounds both results, since a stream-interleaving effect reverses sign with state sharing (+3.5 to -2.4 pp). An aggregate score can hide individual harm, as [1] showed, but it cannot distinguish a uniform loss from a deleted class; class-level change should therefore be reported alongside the aggregate and subject-level change alongside it, with both results conditional on the cell, the rule and the stream construction.
  </details>

- **[When Cooperation Must Change: Semantic Strategy Unlearning in Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17917)**  `arXiv:2611.17917`  
  _Wenhan Wu, Linghan Chen, Yudong Gao, Fan Yang, Honglong Chen, Kai Yin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) supports coordinated decision-making in distributed Web services, multi-robot control, and collaborative games through learned coordination conventions. When deployment requirements change, a previously useful convention may become obsolete or unsafe. For example, revised cache-sharing permissions in Web content delivery may require caches to abandon a learned division of storage and serving roles while maintaining efficient delivery. Teams must therefore remove the specified convention while preserving useful cooperation. This is challenging because the same relational pattern can be reproduced by different agents across different histories. Runtime shielding leaves the underlying coordination preference intact and can impair task progress, while existing reinforcement unlearning and trajectory-deletion methods do not directly target role-invariant conventions. This paper formalizes this problem as semantic coordination-strategy unlearning and proposesthis http URLuses causal coordination attribution to identify influential interactions and retain-aware subspace updates to limit interference with unrelated behaviors. Counterfactual re-coordination helps agents learn productive alternatives, while adversarial auditing tests whether the target convention can still be recovered. Accepted updates are merged into decentralized actors for direct deployment. Evaluation across five cooperative MARL benchmarks shows that CARVE reduces mean nominal convention-completion and worst-case elicitation rates by 91.4\% and 83.3\%,this http URLmedian utility loss is 5.9\%, and measured inference latency matches that of the original policies.
  </details>

- **[SWIFT: Sample-Wise Instance Fine-Tuning of Time-Series Foundation Models for Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17915)**  `arXiv:2611.17915`  
  _Yang Chen, Feifan Tian, Cong Wang, Cheng Feng, Wenbin Li, Yang Gao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While current few-shot adaptation strategies for time series forecasting foundation models are predominantly dataset-centric, the necessity of obtaining an additional dataset for the finetuning process poses a significant hurdle for adoption. To address this constraint, we propose Sample-Wise Instance Fine-Tuning (SWIFT), a novel forecasting adaptation framework that achieves fine-grained specialization by leveraging structural priors from the target instance's own history. This shift allows the fine-tuning process to be entirely transparent to the end-user, which significantly enhances the ease of adoption. To implement this framework, SWIFT synergizes Self-Retrieval for historical mining and Frequency-Aware Loss for spectral consistency, while employing Deviation-Aware Dynamic Fusion to balance adaptation with general knowledge preservation. Extensive experiments across four TSFM backbones and three long-history benchmark subsets validate SWIFT, yielding average MASE and WQL reductions of 6.41% and 4.83% across 24 combinations of four backbones and six Chronos Benchmark II datasets. To the best of our knowledge, SWIFT is the first framework to investigate sample-wise instance fine-tuning of time-series foundation models, specializing the model for an individual prediction window using only the corresponding forecasting sample's own observed history.
  </details>

- **[Adversarially Attacking Symbolic Vocabulary Vulnerabilities In LLM Planners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17910)**  `arXiv:2611.17910`  
  _Stephen Obadinma, Xiaodan Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents, their abilities in navigating automated planning tasks have become essential. However, despite their strengths, LLMs' planning ability is not robust, particularly when they are not allowed to exploit commonsense cues in the symbolic vocabulary (e.g., names of actions, predicates, and objects) used to define planning tasks. This makes LLMs unreliable compared to symbolic planners. With recent reasoning models showing improved planning performance on tasks with obfuscated symbolic vocabularies, we utilize an adversarial attack framework to reveal to what extent the crucial weaknesses of LLM agents at joint symbolic interpretation and planning remain and whether they can reason properly when presented with severe cases. As such, our main contribution is devising an LLM-based attack model, which we call Symbolic Swapper, to automatically generate obfuscated planning domains with non-standard symbolic vocabularies that systematically break strong reasoning models' ability to plan. In doing so, we reveal how much LLMs' planning abilities can be further degraded under more advanced obfuscation schemes, allowing us to ascertain their success when having to rely on their pure planning ability. We find that attacks can decrease the planning performance of base models and even robust planning frameworks by over 70%. We further analyze the factors behind how LLM planning abilities break down and find that successful obfuscation causes models to appear to significantly degrade in reasoning quality despite them showing some inherent ability to understand the domain, revealing a gap in the perception of a task domain and being able to plan under it.
  </details>

- **[OmniScore-Bench: Evaluating Vision-Language Models on Musical Score Reading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17870)**  `arXiv:2611.17870`  
  _Congren Dai, Zhancheng Guo, Danni Zhao, Wenchu Wang, Xiaobing Li, Maosong Sun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can answer questions about musical scores, but their ability to recognise notation, recover its structure and interpret its musical meaning requires separate evaluation. Existing optical music recognition (OMR) resources establish detection and transcription measurements; a transcription score alone leaves the strengths and bottlenecks of a VLM score reader unresolved. We introduce OmniScore-Bench, an evaluation of OMR capabilities in VLMs through 3,000 questions spanning six aspects and 24 task subtypes. Each question specifies a visible scope, reference annotations and an answer schema, enabling automatic evaluation from symbol localisation to musical consistency and evidence-linked question answering. Our assessment of twelve VLMs reveals operation-specific capability profiles and different transcription leaders across excerpt, single-page and multipage tasks. Controlled analyses examine the roles of score images, auxiliary givens and additional page context. On the tested subset, image removal exposes visual dependence in pitch and repeat interpretation while measuring how much performance remains with public context alone. Separate format and content analyses distinguish answer-format violations from musical errors. OmniScore-Bench supports diagnosing which score-reading operations VLMs perform reliably and which require improvement.
  </details>

- **[FlexCast: Flexible Context Learning for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17862)**  `arXiv:2611.17862`  
  _Zuyang Shen, Luqi Gong, Fanda Fan, Bingsong Wang, Xiang Ao, Yahui Wei, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models (TSFMs) based on autoregressive (AR) next-token prediction entangle two factors: variable-context conditioning and prediction-unit factorization with autoregressive rollout. Strong AR performance therefore does not reveal whether generalization stems from the flexible training distribution, the local prediction target, the left-to-right inference process, or their combination. We propose FlexCast, a flexible context-target learning framework that disentangles these factors. FlexCast reformulates forecasting as variable-length masked prediction over time series patches: during pretraining, it dynamically samples visible context and masked target patches, exposing the model to diverse context-target configurations without sequential decoding. At inference time, the same pretrained model supports one-step forecasting, confidence-based iterative refinement, and next-token-style generation through a unified mask-and-predict interface, enabling controlled comparison of inference schedules under identical architecture, data, and objective. Evaluated on BLAST and multiple zero-shot benchmarks, FlexCast achieves accuracy competitive with or better than strong TSFM baselines while substantially reducing inference latency. Ablations confirm that context diversity and contiguous target masking drive performance, while left-to-right decoding does not yield consistent gains once flexible context-target relationships have been learned, suggesting that the key benefit of AR-style TSFMs lies in flexible training rather than sequential inference. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AI-aided Decision-Making in Education - Uncertainty, Explainability, and Human Responsibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17858)**  `arXiv:2611.17858`  
  _Fabian Kieser, Paul Tschisgale, Peter Wulff_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based systems have become increasingly integrated into various aspects of everyday life, including education, understanding how these systems make decisions is of paramount importance. AI holds significant potential to enhance educational practices through the facilitation of personalized and adaptive learning, automation of assessment procedures, and assistance with administrative tasks. However, the opacity of AI-based decision-making in education (especially with deep learning and generative AI models) can hamper decision-making, especially when outcomes appear unjustified or incorrect. In this chapter, we argue that three key principles (uncertainty, explainability, and human responsibility) are essential for the legitimate use of AI in educational settings. Acknowledging the inherent uncertainties in AI-based decisions, analogous to those in human decision-making, is fundamental. Explainability is critical for ensuring that the functioning and outcomes of AI applications are transparent and comprehensible to both educators and learners. Furthermore, human responsibility is crucial, particularly in human decision-making, which remains indispensable across all stages of AI development and deployment. By examining these three principles, this chapter provides a comprehensive guide for stakeholders seeking to integrate AI thoughtfully and effectively into the educational system.
  </details>

- **[Aligning AI Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17820)**  `arXiv:2611.17820`  
  _Siddarth Srinivasan, Morgan Jane Matthews, Jascha Sohl-Dickstein, Erik Jones_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Previous work has shown that alignment training on individual LLM agents can fail to transfer to multi-agent settings. We investigate the underlying mechanisms for this failure, and explore interventions to preserve alignment transfer to multi-agent LLM teams. We focus on two software-engineering tasks (sepsis triage, a news recommender) and ten consulting-proposal generation tasks. We present four findings: (1) The single-vs-team safety gap does not show a clear trend with more capable models. We find that Anthropic's Mythos Preview shows the largest gap on one of our tasks but the smallest on another; (2) Many natural interventions, such as having agents critique each other's work or coordinate in a group chat, do not reliably bring agent teams back to single-agent alignment behavior; (3) diffusion of responsibility substantially accounts for the safety gap: agents in teams are less likely to proactively check for system-level safety failures and more likely to ignore, rationalize, or deflect responsibility for issues when they arise; (4) having a designated safety lead agent ensure system-level safety, and having a coordinator agent publish a safety specifications document are effective at restoring single-agent alignment. We also release magelab, a multi-agent LLM orchestration and experimentation framework. Our findings can help practitioners design agent teams that successfully preserve the behaviors expected of aligned individual agents.
  </details>

- **[Causally Constrained and Uncertainty-Aware Cardiopulmonary Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17819)**  `arXiv:2611.17819`  
  _Yuxiang Lai, Jiashuo Zhang, Liang Zhao, Xiaofeng Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Routine chest computed tomography (CT) captures pulmonary, cardiac, and vascular phenotypes that jointly stratify lung cancer and cardiovascular disease, the two leading causes of death in lung-screening populations. Yet clinical risk calculators evaluate each disease in isolation and omit CT findings, whereas unconstrained machine learning overfits small hospital cohorts, learns treatment- and illness-confounded risk directions, and conceals uncertainty when patient records are incomplete. Here we present the Causal Envelope Agent (CE-Agent), a two-stage framework that compiles peer-reviewed epidemiological evidence into explicit constraints on risk-factor weights before any outcome is seen, while estimating local baseline risk and newly added CT phenotypes from cohort data. At deployment, closed-form uncertainty propagation accounts for missing clinical or CT inputs within milliseconds per patient, running over two orders of magnitude faster than Monte Carlo sampling and returning calibrated risk intervals that direct chart review and selective imaging to borderline patients. Across 53,452 lung-screening participants from 33 US hospitals and 6,814 multi-ethnic adults, CE-Agent outperforms cardiovascular calculators in lung screening, exceeds the Pooled Cohort Equations on hard atherosclerotic events while correcting their 1.7-fold risk overestimation, and generalizes from a single hospital or CT scanner to external sites while lowering required sample sizes up to fivefold. Applied unchanged to lung cancer, CE-Agent matches established screening models with accurate calibration even when restricted to risk-factor directions alone, and pairing both predictions improves joint cardiopulmonary triage from a single screening visit.
  </details>

- **[Can Agents Keep Up with Time? An Empirical Analysis of Temporal Adaptation After a Conversational Hiatus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17806)**  `arXiv:2611.17806`  
  _Hongguan Su, Ming Wang, Shun Zhang, Yifan Wang, Rong Yin, Zhiming Ma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agents increasingly serve as persistent personal assistants across modern web platforms, managing schedules, applications, and multi-step plans over weeks or months. Users often return to an ongoing conversation after a long conversational hiatus, while earlier bookings and preparation notes stay on record. During this hiatus, scheduled dates may pass even though the retained records remain unchanged and event outcomes go unrecorded. Existing evaluations of long-horizon agents focus on fact retention and explicit state updates. Whether agents perceive the passage of time and adapt their advice accordingly therefore remains unknown. We frame this ability as temporal state perception and use, which means perceiving the current time and applying it when interpreting dated records. To study it, we design session-rekindling tasks that keep the earlier conversation fixed and vary only the follow-up date and the way temporal information reaches the agent. Experiments across five model routes and five temporal-information conditions show that agents can obtain the current time but often fail to use it. They reliably query the clock and even note in their reasoning that the scheduled date has passed, yet many still tell users to prepare for the expired event. Supplying explicit temporal context reduces this error, and providing the computed date relation removes it entirely. Keeping up with time therefore depends on turning perceived time into updated plans. Based on these findings, we design a session-rekindling benchmark that provides temporal-awareness guidance for designing long-horizon agents. The code can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Vision-as-a-Tool: Visual Harness for Multimodal Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17797)**  `arXiv:2611.17797`  
  _Haolin Yang, Wenshan Wu, Jin Jiang, Yu Cheng, Yan Xia, Ting Song, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agentic coding requires robust visual reasoning to analyze observations and evaluate generated artifacts. Current frameworks largely rely on a Thinking-with-Images paradigm, where agents depend on their intrinsic vision to execute visually grounded tasks. However, this architectural coupling introduces a fundamental bottleneck. Agent performance remains strictly bounded by internal perceptual limitations. Furthermore, text-only agents lack native perception. Without specialized tools, they cannot perform visual reasoning. To overcome these constraints, we introduce the Vision-as-a-Tool (VaaT) paradigm. Prior visual tool-use merely manipulates images for an agent's intrinsic vision to read. In contrast, VaaT reframes general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as callable external utilities. As a result, text-only agents can completely offload visual reasoning during agentic execution. Meanwhile, vision-capable agents can dynamically augment their native perception on demand. We instantiate this paradigm in VHarness, a unified framework driven by five atomic actions. We comprehensively evaluate VHarness across Image-to-HTML, Image-to-SVG, and Image-to-3D generation tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that VHarness consistently improves visual fidelity and geometric accuracy across both text-only and multimodal coding agents. These findings highlight that modularizing visual reasoning into callable utilities provides a flexible and scalable foundation for future agentic coding systems.
  </details>

- **[Robustness Evaluation of GPU Cloud Pricing Benchmarks under Incomplete Observations: A Controlled Missingness Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17796)**  `arXiv:2611.17796`  
  _Jooya Wong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Daily GPU rental-price benchmarks can fail when marketplace quotes are missing. This article stress-tests a fixed equal-weight median rule under four imposed masking mechanisms and four benchmark-level recovery methods. Across 5,800 Monte Carlo realisations in 27 scenarios on 190 held-out days, whole-source outages reduce publication coverage to 0.77 while preserving zero conditional error on published dates; composition-changing loss retains 0.86 coverage but produces 7.74 log points of error. A separate past-only stratum-level ablation reduces error by 4.15 to 6.70 log points in five composition-changing cases, using a richer information set. An observable-signal withholding rule improves the primary error-coverage criterion in 0 of 10 cases. Results concern observed asking-price measurements, not transaction prices or identified real-world missingness mechanisms.
  </details>

- **[MASTARS: Multi-Agent Sequential Trajectory Augmentation with Return-Conditioned Subgoals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17793)**  `arXiv:2611.17793`  
  _Jiwon Jeon, Myungsik Cho, Woojun Kim, Seongmin Kim, Woohyeon Byeon, Seungyul Han, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The performance of offline Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) is limited by the quality of the fixed offline dataset, motivating trajectory augmentation. However, augmenting multi-agent trajectories presents a fundamental challenge of the diversity-coordination trade-off: independent generation improves diversity but lacks coordination, while joint generation induces coordination but lacks diversity, reproducing the training distribution. To address this challenge in multi-agent data augmentation, we introduce MASTARS, a novel diffusion-based framework that generates multi-agent trajectories sequentially across agents while enforcing cross-agent consistent coordination via the method of inpainting. MASTARS produces trajectories that are diverse, coordinated and globally coherent. Experiments on various benchmarks such as MPE, SMAC, and SMAC-v2 demonstrate significant performance gains across multiple offline MARL methods.
  </details>

- **[AutoRL: A Multi-Agent System towards Autonomous Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17790)**  `arXiv:2611.17790`  
  _Simon Tang, Wenjie Shi, Yongjing Yin, Keheng Wang, Jialin Liu, Zheng Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is one of the most promising paths toward an AI singularity for superintelligence. Achieving this vision requires AI systems to undertake the full training pipeline for iterative improvement. Reinforcement learning (RL) plays an essential role in this cycle to improve model capabilities and generalization, yet automating RL remains difficult due to unstable training dynamics and the need for sustained supervision. We introduce AutoRL, a multi-agent system for automating RL training, as a cornerstone for future RSI. Different from conducting research by end-to-end complete experiments, AutoRL monitors the evolving training state and integrates process-level supervision and research into an ongoing RL training, following the practical workflow of an RL researcher. Specifically, AutoRL consists of three RL-specific agentic roles. Perceptors track training dynamics, rollouts, evaluation, and efficiency, triggering deliberation when they detect anomalies, degradation, or stagnation. Three complementary Proposers diagnose problems and refine candidate interventions through shared evidence and checks. The Orchestrator reviews their recommendation and applies approved changes through online hot updates or checkpoint-based resumption. Experiments on Qwen3 and LongCat-Flash-Lite demonstrate the superiority of AutoRL over static RL recipes and a general agent. The research records show that existing agents can handle unattended RL supervision and dynamic adjustment. The difficulty for future RSI lies in developing fundamental methods that are valuable for resource-consuming validation.
  </details>

- **[TReC: Evaluating Move-Induced State Changes in Instruction-Conditioned Go](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17781)**  `arXiv:2611.17781`  
  _Zhuangsheng Li, Yunhan Zhang, Dailin Li, Qingxian Yu, Yu Wu, Youqi Song, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a Go model predicts a move different from the expert's, coordinate distance measures how far the prediction lands from the reference. We introduce Transition Representation Consistency (TReC) to compare how the two moves change a board-state embedding. Both moves are executed from the same position under the game rules, and TReC is the cosine similarity between the two resulting representation changes. In experiments with a frozen KataGo network on 680 mid-to-late-game positions from five games, the gap between the highest and lowest candidate per position has a median of 0.01 to 0.08 for the direct successor-state cosine and 0.88 to 1.33 for TReC, depending on the evaluator layer. TReC follows coordinate distance only weakly: the rank correlation between a candidate's distance to the reference and its TReC, computed within each position, has a median of -0.19 over these positions.
  </details>

- **[Certified Improvements to Best-Known Thomson-Problem Configurations with a Search Pipeline Built with Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17776)**  `arXiv:2611.17776`  
  _Hui Shao, Tao Wang, Xiaotian Liu, Yijie Peng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Thomson problem asks for N unit charges on a sphere with minimum Coulomb energy; for large N the best configurations known are curated in the Cambridge Cluster Database, whose largest-N entries were set in October 2026. We report configurations for N = 912, 1382 and 2502 with energies 0.0036186, 0.0109730 and 0.0614033 below the database values, certified by exact integer arithmetic. All three are numerically strict local minima modulo rotations and keep the defect inventory and scar compositions of the database configurations; the database value for N = 912 had been reached by two different methods. Our search runs unconstrained replica-exchange basin hopping from the database configurations, with a shared pool of minima, recombination of improved regions after relabelling by geometry, and gradient-only polishing; replica-exchange basin hopping alone found the N = 912 and 1382 configurations, the former in one of five runs. We built and ran it with large language model agents (Claude, through a chat interface and the Claude Code agent), and we report every search, including those that failed. Two findings matter for others who search the database. Polishing alone lowers two entries by more than the threshold at which our pipeline reports a candidate, which produced two spurious records until candidates were compared with the polished database energies (the polished baseline). A local-relaxation speed-up passed its exactness test yet found nothing in a controlled comparison in which every arm with full relaxation went below the database value within 15 minutes. We distil three practices for computational discovery with large language model agents: calibrate the search code and verify candidates with code separate from the search, compare candidates with the polished baseline, and test design changes in controlled comparisons with pass criteria fixed in advance.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge After Collapse: Context and Wording Shape Factual Accessibility in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17763)**  `arXiv:2611.17763`  
  _Sujato Dutta, Prithviraj Patil, Chandrajit Bajaj_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a language model accepts a false correction in a factual discussion, its next answer can depend on how the conversation continues. We introduce Knowledge Answer Divergence (KAD), an event-conditioned evaluation protocol that distinguishes initial false-answer adoption from subsequent persistence, recovery, and abstention. KAD freezes the conversation at the first explicit adoption of a designated false target and branches independent probes from that exact history. This enables matched comparisons of post-adoption behavior and history-management interventions that a single susceptibility score cannot capture. We evaluate eight local checkpoint conditions and six hosted endpoints, alongside an audited 300-question rerun. In corrected Llama 3.2 Instruct 3B controls, a neutral-history branch yields 42/46 correct responses, compared with 16/46 under full history. A matched cue-placement control changes false adoption by 27.3 percentage points in Qwen 3.5 4B. Twelve independently authored pressure templates demonstrate substantial sensitivity to wording. Together, these findings demonstrate the utility of KAD for evaluating factual reliability beyond initial susceptibility, enabling systematic comparisons of false-answer persistence, recovery conditions and conversational interventions.
  </details>

- **[A Spectral Framework for Universal Proxy Regularization in Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17728)**  `arXiv:2611.17728`  
  _Shangge Liu, Danyue Qian, Qi Fan, Yinghuan Shi, Wenbin Li, Lei Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model merging constructs multi-task models without expensive joint training but often suffers from parameter interference. Some solutions mitigate this by constraining finetuning using data from the other tasks to be merged, violating the core independence and privacy assumption of model merging. Recent work offers preliminary empirical evidence that a universal, task-agnostic proxy distribution can substitute for such data, but leaves open when and why it suffices. To this end, we propose a \textit{Spectral Framework for Universal Proxy Regularization}, which characterizes the relationship between a proxy distribution and the distribution of other tasks to be merged through spectral coverage ($\alpha$) and leakage ($\beta$). Based on the framework, we derive a unified parameter interference bound: smaller $(\alpha, \beta)$ provably yields lower parameter interference through better spectral alignment. To exploit such a proxy, we augment the training objective with a proxy regularizer. Evaluating this regularizer during fine-tuning requires a concrete mechanism. We study three instantiations, including (J) an exact evaluation via Jacobian-vector products, (F) a frozen curvature approximation using the Fisher information matrix, and (O) an output-drift formulation that constrains the model's functional deviation on the proxy directly. We prove that all three coincide under the NTK linearization, where existing analyses of such regularizers stop, and go further to characterize standard non-linear fine-tuning, bounding how far each instantiation departs from its linearized reference and obtain a three-way hierarchy, zero for (J), proxy-independent for (F), and proxy-quality-scaling for (O). Empirical validation on the commonly used vision-language model CLIP confirms these theoretical predictions.
  </details>

- **[Federated Soft Poly-Level Learning Over a Distributed Network with Guaranteed Convergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17722)**  `arXiv:2611.17722`  
  _Yang Jiao, Kai Yang, Jianwei Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-level learning/optimization, e.g., bilevel optimization, has been recognized for its versatile applicability across a broad array of domains, spanning from signal processing to machine learning. However, most existing works focus on bilevel and trilevel optimization. In contrast, multi-level optimization problems involving four or more hierarchical levels, which we term poly-level learning (PLL) problems, remain largelythis http URLaddition, in practical PLL, data may be distributed across multiple nodes over a distributed network. Nevertheless, extant studies on PLL predominantly focus on the non-federated setting, necessitating the collection of data to central servers and thereby posing potential privacy risks. To this end, we propose a Federated Poly-Level Learning (FPLL) framework over a distributed network to tackle the PLL problems with soft constraints in a federated manner. FPLL hinges on a novel non-linear cut, i.e., $L$-cut, for the level-wise relaxation to the PLL problems. Additionally, the proposed federated algorithm is designed with adjustable parameters that allow for a flexible trade-off between its computational complexity and performance efficiency, enabling users to tune it according to specific application requirements. We also carry out non-asymptotic analysis to derive the iteration complexity for the proposed FPLL to achieve the $\epsilon$-Pareto stationary point. Extensive experiments have been undertaken to elucidate the superior efficacy of the proposed method.
  </details>

- **[Co-Sight Harness: Externalized Control Assets and Deterministic Enforcement for Long-Horizon Business Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17696)**  `arXiv:2611.17696`  
  _Luyao Wang, Xijun Zhu, Zhaohui Hao, Xiwen Zhang, Xingyu Wen, Weishan Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon business workflows fail in two ways: errors accumulate as a chain of activities lengthens, and the knowledge an activity needs is written in a different vocabulary from the user's request. We study whether such workflows can be made reliable without changing the language model. Our method keeps the backbone fixed and moves the workflow definition, state and domain knowledge into versioned control assets outside the model. A state machine and machine-checkable acceptance gates enforce these assets during execution; an independent auditor grades the resulting trace. The assets are updated only after a regression check and human approval .We evaluate the method on three deployed workflows exceeding 200 steps. On Domain A, a 120-case end-to-end evaluation set shows unattended completion rising from 50% to 100%, retrieval recall from 56% to 91.67%, and end-to-end latency dropping from 440 to 159 minutes, with scenario-analysis recall rising from 45% to 93%. Two other domains were taken over by teams that did not write the assets, one in four days and one in seven days. A controlled ablation separates the two failure sources. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Aligned, Conflicting, or Unspecified? A Diagnostic Benchmark for Multimodal Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17680)**  `arXiv:2611.17680`  
  _Xintong Wang, Haihan Nan, Ruidong Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial Intelligence of Things (AIoT) systems increasingly combine sensed time series with external text such as expert reports and news, yet how reliably models use text that agrees or conflicts with the sensed history remains unclear. We introduce the first real-world diagnostic benchmark organized by text-history relationship: 1,762 text-window pairs across sensor telemetry (Weather), web-service traffic (Wikipedia), and market data streams (Finance), each labeled Aligned, Conflicting, or Unspecified by whether the text's direction agrees with the historical trend, opposes it, or is not stated. Across ten pretrained models evaluated zero-shot, we investigate text selection and combination under text-history relationships (RQ1) and error diagnosis and horizon-scaled revision control (RQ2). For RQ1, we compare two types of text on paired Weather windows: one directly forecasts temperature changes, while the other describes related weather conditions, such as winds and fronts. Under Aligned, the first type yields the lowest error for most models. Under Conflicting, it increases most models' error above the no-text baseline; we therefore add only its stated magnitude and timing to the second type. Compared with either type alone, this combination lowers mean absolute scaled error (MASE) for six models and raises directional accuracy (DA) for seven. For RQ2, an error decomposition attributes the error increase to oversized revisions, and a training-free rule that limits them outperforms forecasts both with and without text for the four models with this trade-off, lowering MASE by 4.49%-21.98% and raising DA by 2.99%-9.71%. Our dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learn then Bet: Learning Interpretable Rules for Reliable Agent Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17662)**  `arXiv:2611.17662`  
  _You-Lin Chen, Xinyi Lisa Chen, Pedro Herrero-Vidal, Xiaojing Yu, Bingqing Song, Bin Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous agents now run for hundreds of steps with little or no human supervision. In practice, practitioners use runtime monitors such as turn limits and keyword filters to decide when a run should be stopped, upgraded, or assisted. However, these heuristics may stop runs that would otherwise succeed or allow failing runs to continue. To address this limitation, we introduce LeBet (Learn then Bet), a statistical learning framework that derives its learning objective from sufficient conditions for valid and powerful intervention. In particular, LeBet learns an interpretable OR-of-AND monitor and accumulates evidence through a fixed betting statistic, intervening when the evidence reaches a prescribed threshold. Under finite within-class exchangeability and leave-one-out stability, we derive finite-sample guarantees for false-positive control (validity) and detection power conditional on the training data, using the same trajectories for learning and calibration without assuming independent and identically distributed trajectories. To address the intractable combinatorial optimization problem in our learning formulation, we use stochastic local search that leverages the monitor's Boolean structure to propose rule edits aimed at improving empirical separation. On SWE-bench Lite with Ministral 3 8B, the learned monitor saves $34.46\%$ of input-plus-output tokens and increases output precision from $18.33\%$ to $21.59\%$. The empirical false-positive rate is $13.64\%$. Our results show how to formalize the learning problem over interpretable rule sets with statistical conditions and establish training-conditional guarantees for reliable intervention based on partial histories.
  </details>

- **[Generated Evaluation Suites as Diversity-Driven Search for Real Agent Regressions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17632)**  `arXiv:2611.17632`  
  _Pulkit Yadav_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating an evaluation suite from an agent's specification is diversity-driven search: a generator fills a descriptor space (requirements $\times$ personas $\times$ scenario kinds), and the suite it emits is an archive, then scored like a benchmark by its mean pass rate, which throws away the search's product. We inject 7 controlled defects into a $\tau^2$-bench agent across two domains. Coverage yields a ceiling: if a defect's traced footprint holds $b$ scenarios that pass at baseline, it can itself contribute at most $b/N$ to a suite of $N$. For 4 of 5 traceable defects that ceiling lies at or below the detection threshold $\theta = 0.10$, so no within-footprint effect can move the suite past it; in both domains the tool-removal defect destroys every covered scenario it can (exactly its ceiling) while the suite-level pass rate moves the other way. Defects split into two regimes: trace-aligned ones, where per-cell scoring recovers the tool-removal defect in full and reveals a second as a significant improvement, and trace-orthogonal ones, whose footprint is behavioural: forced escalation is detected in retail at a traced coverage of exactly zero. Diversity along the generator's own axes helps only where it aligns: at fixed suite size a happy-path-only suite detects nothing, while a kind-diverse one detects the orchestration defect in 5 of 20 draws. Evaluation archives should be scored per cell and should publish coverage as a first-class property.
  </details>

- **[Safety Side Effects of Preventative Persona Steering: An Empirical Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17620)**  `arXiv:2611.17620`  
  _Sabrina Tang, Nikolai Vo, Carson Murillo, Chira Sukumarnpiti, Sarah Wilson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-level behaviors such as sycophancy and hallucination correspond to directions in a model's activation space (Chen et al., 2025). These directions, known as persona vectors, can suppress undesirable traits either at inference time, subtracting the vector during generation, or through preventative steering, adding the vector during fine-tuning, so the optimizer never needs to encode the trait in weights, then removing the vector at evaluation. Chen et al. (2025) recommend preventative steering because it preserves Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) accuracy, but validations of this recommendation have measured capability alone. We primarily evaluate results on preventative steering of sycophancy and hallucination vectors on three models, measuring three safety benchmarks alongside trait expression, coherence, and MMLU. Our findings show that preventative steering still changes refusal behavior, but by an amount that depends on the trait and does not establish that preventative persona steering is safety-neutral, and steering methods should be evaluated on several safety benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[LLM-Guided Cluster Lifecycle Management: Deciding When and How to Evolve Behavioral Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17616)**  `arXiv:2611.17616`  
  _Himanshu Kulkarni, Siddharth Krishnamurthy, Jianhong Xin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Behavioral clusters learned over user or item interactions drift as behavior evolves: some fragment into sub-behaviors, some converge, some disappear, and new patterns emerge. Two paradigms dominate the maintenance of evolving clusterings. Threshold triggers fire a pre-specified action when a hardcoded statistic crosses a cutoff; refit-and-track (MONIC-style) re-clusters every window and infers transitions from how the new clusters align with the previous window's. Both hardwire the decision into a threshold or a re-clustering rule. We study an alternative in which a large language model (LLM) makes the lifecycle decision in place. Given a threshold-free, whole-population view of the current cluster set (statistical health, labels, member texts, residual mass), the LLM decides per cluster whether and how to act (split, merge, retire, create, or no-action) without re-clustering. Because the useful signal channel depends on how informative the labels are, we fix a richness-conditioned policy in advance: statistics when label distinctiveness is low, fused statistics+semantics when it is high. On three public datasets spanning label richness (YooChoose 0.17, MOOC 0.50, News-Category 0.70), the policy-selected LLM mode obtains the highest F1 on all three, with far fewer interventions and stronger restraint than tuned threshold heuristics and a MONIC steelman (paired Wilcoxon p <= 0.003, bootstrap 95% CIs excluding zero). The gain is concentrated more in precision than recall. The useful modality shifts from statistics to semantics/fusion as richness rises. Semantics hurt on YooChoose, fusion is roughly neutral on MOOC, and the semantic channel is required on News. One base prompt template and one fixed policy serve all three datasets, whereas the tuned baseline collapses when moved between them. We treat this shift as a practitioner diagnostic observed on three datasets, not a validated law.
  </details>

- **[When Does Less Information Pay? An Exact Finite Atlas of Representation, Payoff, and Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17583)**  `arXiv:2611.17583`  
  _Aleksandr M. Shtraukh-Adamsky, Vladimir M. Adamsky, Irina G. Voloshina, Artemiy D. Spitsyn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A representation that retains one bit about a four-state world can solve a task perfectly, while another that retains 1.5 bits on average reaches only 75% accuracy. The difference lies in which distinctions each representation preserves. We examine this relationship in a uniform four-state world by enumerating all admissible representations and binary action rules. Constructions are compared both by replacing one component while holding the others fixed and by choosing the best action rule separately for each module. We consider three tasks, their mixtures, and four separate hypothetical cost tariffs.In some comparisons, the representation retaining less world information has the higher payoff; in others, the more informative representation does. There are also pairs with different information amounts but equal payoff--that is, equal task accuracy. If a representation preserves all existing distinctions and adds new ones, its best zero-cost payoff cannot decrease. Lower-information advantages therefore depend on which distinctions are retained. Equal counts of opposing advantages under representation replacement follow from coding symmetry. In broader comparisons, the shares depend on how repeated realizations are counted. A different task set or resource price can change which member of a fixed pair performs better.These results are exact, complete censuses of the specified finite sets of constructions and comparisons. The atlas provides a reproducible worked model for examining how action constraints, counting rules, and cost assumptions affect comparisons.
