To avoid such redundancy, we propose MacroGS, a feed-forward decoder that jointly predicts a single shared Gaussian set per macro pixel rather than generating separate sets for each view. Disparity-dependent replacement and angle-conditioned appearance allow each set to be reused across all output views, keeping the predicted primitive count independent of the number of views at each requested scale. The decoder iteratively predicts per-primitive disparities, retrieves the corresponding multi-view features, and updates the primitive states. Gaussian splatting and residual refinement then reconstruct the output light field. Despite being learned without geometric supervision, the predicted disparities strongly correlate with underlying scene geometry. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that MacroGS integrates effectively with diverse light field encoders, improving reconstruction quality at fixed scales while achieving leading performance across most evaluated arbitrary-scale settings. The decoder also achieves speedups of approximately $2$-$10\times$ over state-of-the-art implicit light field decoders and $10$-$18\times$ over the view-wise Gaussian baseline.
  </details>

- **[nnAEU: a Simple and Efficient CNN-Autoencoder for Hyperspectral Unmixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17956)**  `arXiv:2611.17956`  
  _Edgard Dabier, Christophe Kervazo, Pietro Gori, Florence Tupin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning-based hyperspectral unmixing (HSU) has followed a trajectory of increasing architectural complexity, from simple autoencoders to transformers and, most recently, Mamba-based models, each claiming to advance the state of the art. Contrary to this trend, we propose a lightweight CNN-based autoencoder, coined "Not New Autoencoder Unmixing" (nnAEU). It is derived from the backbone of UNMamba (2025) through a pruning study that removes the Mamba component entirely. nnAEU uses less than 10 times fewer parameters than UNMamba, and far fewer than other transformer- and GAN-based methods, while outperforming them in accuracy. We provide a complete, minimal, reproducible code, available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Domain Adaptation Based Pipeline for Character Classification and Handwritten Text Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17952)**  `arXiv:2611.17952`  
  _Florent Imbert, Simon Corbillé, Hui Han, Elisa H. Barney Smith_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Character classification and handwritten text recognition, particularly in specialized tasks, often suffer from limited labeled data, while a large amount of unlabeled data remains unused. To address this challenge, we propose a novel unsupervised domain adaptation-based pipeline to reduce the distribution gap between source and target domains. Our pipeline follows three main steps. In the first step, we pretrained an expert model on source data, to learn a strong general representation. In the second step, we perform unsupervised domain adaptation to leverage unlabeled target data, adapting the model to the specific characteristics of the target domain. In the third step, we trained the expert model on source data in the target style. By aligning the feature distributions between the source and target, our method enables the model to generalize effectively across diverse datasets. The unsupervised training increases the computation time only during training not in inference. We evaluate the pipeline on both character-level and historical handwritten line recognition tasks, demonstrating its flexibility. We establish a new state-of-the-art on the Safran-MNIST-DLS dataset defined in Track 2 of the DAGECC competition with 76.17% Macro F1-score and achieve competitive results on each of the specific datasets of READ 2018 without using the labels of the target data. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Token2Tile: Token-Wise Sparse Attention with Tile Execution through a Low-Rank Space for Video DiTs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17930)**  `arXiv:2611.17930`  
  _Xuecheng Wu, Aichen Feng, Jinyan Chen, Qingjie Zhao, Julien Lai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attention is essential for modeling spatio-temporal dependencies in video diffusion transformers, but its quadratic cost makes it a major inference bottleneck. Its high sparsity offers an opportunity to accelerate generation by focusing computation on important interactions. However, accuracy and execution favor different granularities: each query attends to its own key tokens, whereas GPU kernels work on tiles. We present Token2Tile, a training-free sparse attention operator that combines per-query scoring and a per-query residual with tile execution. An online low-rank space, computed directly from queries and keys to preserve attention logits, makes clustering, scoring, and residual approximation efficient. Token2Tile unites query-specific selections to allocate exact work across query clusters, then packs the selected keys into GPU tiles. On MiniMax-H3 and Wan2.1-14B, Token2Tile delivers 1.48-1.54x end-to-end speedup over dense FlashAttention-3 with VBench scores within 0.5 points of dense. At comparable speeds on H3, it improves PSNR over SOL and SubBlock by 5.5-6.8 dB; on Wan, it exceeds SOL by 4.6-4.8 dB while running faster.
  </details>

- **[Vessel2Brain: Edge-Aware Multi-Scale Flow Matching for 3D Brain Image Generation from Vessel Masks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17919)**  `arXiv:2611.17919`  
  _Zhi Chen, Tianyang Zhang, Theodoros N. Arvanitis, Le Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating realistic 3D brain MRI volumes from sparse anatomical priors is a highly ill-posed yet clinically valuable problem, with important applications in data augmentation, simulation, and downstream neuroimaging analysis. In this work, we investigate the challenging task of synthesising full 3D brain MRI volumes conditioned solely on vascular label maps. While vascular structures provide strong anatomical and topological cues, they occupy only a small fraction of the brain volume and offer no direct information about surrounding tissue appearance. To address this challenge, we propose Vessel2Brain, an edge-aware multi-scale conditional flow matching framework that explicitly incorporates vascular structure information into the generative process. Our approach formulates vessel-guided brain image synthesis as a conditional continuous flow, enabling stable training and flexible generation while preserving structural correspondence. A multi-scale flow matching strategy is introduced to jointly model global anatomical coherence and fine-grained local details, with auxiliary velocity predictions providing additional supervision across spatial resolutions. Furthermore, we design an edge-aware objective that emphasises vascular boundaries during training, encouraging anatomically faithful tissue synthesis around vessel structures. We evaluate Vessel2Brain on two public 3D brain MRI datasets with paired vascular annotations. Extensive quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms state-of-the-art generative models in terms of reconstruction accuracy, structural consistency, and distributional realism.
  </details>

- **[CardioTrace: Motion-Aware Cine Synthesis via Coupled Tracing and Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17909)**  `arXiv:2611.17909`  
  _Zhi Chen, Le Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cardiac cine MRI provides time-resolved visualization of cardiac motion and is essential for evaluating ventricular function and anatomy. In routine clinical acquisition, however, cine sequences are frequently temporally sparse or degraded by arrhythmia, motion, and scan-time constraints, which can hinder reliable quantification and downstream analysis. We present CardioTrace, a motion-aware cine synthesis framework that generates a full cardiac cine sequence from limited input while explicitly modeling and tracing cardiac motion. CardioTrace couples two complementary pathways: a pixel-translation branch that directly predicts cine frames in a residual formulation, and a flow-field branch that estimates time-resolved deformation fields from a reference phase to provide interpretable motion correspondences across frames. To further preserve anatomical plausibility, a segmentation branch supervises clinically meaningful cardiac structures at key phases, and a cross-branch consistency objective encourages the synthesized appearance dynamics to follow the explicit motion guidance, mitigating temporal drift and unrealistic shape transitions over long sequences. The framework also supports prompt-style conditioning to incorporate auxiliary patient or acquisition information, improving robustness across heterogeneous multi-center data. Experiments on public cardiac cine MRI datasets demonstrate that CardioTrace improves perceptual quality and temporal coherence over representative GAN- and diffusion-based baselines, while better preserving ventricular morphology throughout the cardiac cycle. \textit{Our codes are available after review}.
  </details>

- **[Cracks in Embeddings: Robustness and Stability of JEPA-Based Chest X-Ray Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17905)**  `arXiv:2611.17905`  
  _Zahid Mansur, Kalpana Yadav, Deepak Mishra_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) models are increasingly used for medical imaging, however there is a limited understanding about robustness of these models toward real-world image changes. We test four JEPA-based chest X-ray encoders, trained on 78,468 NIH ChestX-ray14 images, under six tests: two black-box adversarial attacks (SPSA, Boundary Attack), three image corruptions (Gaussian noise, brightness shift, contrast shift), and a label-scarcity test, measuring embedding change and classification AUROC. These perturbations cause severe, model-dependent damage: encoder-level Cosine Similarity drops as low as 0.33, and AUROC falls by as much as 0.12 in the worst case (I-JEPA under the Boundary Test). We test two ways of training the small predictor on top of each frozen encoder to address this. Adversarial fine-tuning, trained on clean and perturbed images together, raises embedding consistency above 0.97 in nearly every case and improves AUROC, relative to the same perturbed baseline, for most models, with the largest gain of $+0.10$ on I-JEPA. A Vector-Quantized codebook method, trained only on clean images and evaluated on perturbed test images for the first time, instead degrades under distortion for all four models, most noticeably for I-JEPA ($-0.15$ AUROC on average), showing that its discrete bottleneck alone does not provide the robustness that comes from training on perturbed data directly.
  </details>

- **[Zoom In, Scale Out: Metric Depth Models Mistake Digital Zoom for Moving Closer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17903)**  `arXiv:2611.17903`  
  _Rabeya Noor, Khandoker Nosiba Arifin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many new AI models estimate how far away things are, in metres, from a single photo. To do this they must also work out what camera took the photo. We check whether they really do with a simple test: digital zoom, that is, cropping a photo and enlarging the crop back to full size. Zooming moves nothing in the scene, so a correct model should give the same distance for every point still in the picture. The test is free, exact and needs no labels. Two numbers describe a model's reaction: its depth changes like $s^{-\alpha}$ (ideal $\alpha=0$) and its focal-length estimate like $s^{\beta}$ (ideal $\beta=1$), for zoom factor $s$. On 800 images from KITTI, NYUv2 and DIODE, the widely used Depth Anything V2 has $\alpha=0.95$: it treats a zoomed photo almost as if the camera had moved closer, because it judges distance mainly from how large things look. Models that also predict the camera remove only part of this error ($\alpha\approx0.31$-$0.36$), because their focal estimate grows too little. Given the true camera, Depth Pro and DA3-Nested keep their depth ($\alpha\approx0$), so the error lies in their camera estimate. The reason: a zoomed photo is, pixel for pixel, a normal photo of a scene squashed in depth, so size alone cannot tell the two apart, and a focal estimator trained by regression stays close to the cameras it saw in training. Undoing this needs no labels or training and makes Depth Pro pass the test ($\alpha=0.04$) at almost no cost on normal photos. Controls, including a real optical zoom lens, rule out blur and resizing artefacts. Finally, label-free fine-tuning for self-consistency lowers $\alpha$ to 0.05 on KITTI and 0.07 on NYUv2 once a "blurry means zoomed" shortcut is removed; we report what this costs on normal and unseen photos.
  </details>

- **[See and Detect: Image-Driven Open-Vocabulary Multi-View 3D Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17897)**  `arXiv:2611.17897`  
  _Yating Xu, Na Zhao, Chenhan Jiang, Qingwen Zhang, Jiankang Deng, Jifei Song_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent open-vocabulary multi-view indoor 3D detection methods typically follow a point-cloud-driven pipeline, where pseudo 3D boxes are generated from reconstructed or estimated geometry and semantic labels are inferred from projected regions. However, such a design makes box generation dependent on the completeness of point clouds and restricts semantic learning to local appearance, causing inferior 3D object localization and open-vocabulary classification. In this paper, we present See and Detect, an image-driven framework that uses multi-view images as the primary evidence for 3D box and semantic label generation. For box generation, we first discover object candidates from individual images, enabling objects missing in point clouds to be recovered from visual evidence. However, single-view predictions are often noisy and redundant. To address this, we propose multi-view consensus box consolidation to fuse spatially consistent predictions across views, and box quality assessment to reduce the impact of boxes with partial object coverage. For semantic label generation, we introduce context-aware semantic learning, where semantic labels are queried from full image context, enabling more accurate semantic prediction. Extensive experiments on ScanNet and ARKitScenes demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art open-vocabulary multi-view 3D detection performance while running over 500$\times$ faster than the previous state-of-the-art method.
  </details>

- **[Photogrammetric 3D Model-Based Animal Behavior Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17881)**  `arXiv:2611.17881`  
  _Olaf Hellwich, Moritz Heine, Patrik Reiske, Sole Traverso, Katharina Hohlbaum, Henning Sprekeler, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video-based behavior estimation is often implemented end-to-end from image sequences. We advocate a two-stage alternative: reconstruct 3D body and object geometry before classifying behavior. This makes ambiguities of the ill-posed 2D-to-3D inverse problem explicit and potentially correctable. We demonstrate the strategy with simulated interacting mice and extend it to real video.
  </details>

- **[DySENSE: Dynamic Spatiotemporal ENergy Synthesis and Emulation with Satellite Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17878)**  `arXiv:2611.17878`  
  _Kailai Sun, Zijie Gao, Mingyi He, Can Rong, Alok Prakash, Baoshen Guo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cities account for over two-thirds of the world's energy and more than 70% of global carbon dioxide emissions. City energy is essential for achieving the 2050 net-zero target and the UN's Sustainable Development Goals 7 and 11. Although existing studies have made progress, they are limited by data and methodological gaps. Existing studies are largely predictive for existing cities rather than generative for future cities; fine-grained, aligned monthly dynamic energy data are scarce; and existing GenAI models struggle to generate dynamic urban functional monthly energy. We present DySENSE, a joint multimodal GenAI framework that synthesizes static urban satellite imagery together with spatially aligned, dynamic monthly building energy consumption maps. We decouple long-term static urban morphology from short-term dynamic energy consumption. By conditioning on static urban land use, DySENSE can generate realistic, diverse and new static urban imagery with aligned energy maps. By conditioning on dynamic monthly and climate prompts, DySENSE can jointly generate dynamic, monthly energy consumption maps while keeping the urban morphology static. Experiments across four cities (New York, Washington, Norfolk, Busan) demonstrated that DySENSE can generate annotated high-fidelity synthetic energy data using less than 25% labelled data, boosting downstream prediction performance by 8% mIoU, and achieving strong energy dynamic consistency. Compared to existing methods, DySENSE significantly improved prediction performance (8.7% accuracy) and reduced prediction error (9%-18% CVRMSE, ASHRAE). DySENSE offers an effective foundation for energy-efficient cities, peak-load analysis, building performance simulation and dynamic demand response for city, energy, and building science. The data and code:this https URLandthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Speed-Variation Distillation for Generative Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17868)**  `arXiv:2611.17868`  
  _Sukit Seripanitkarn, Phonphrm Thawatdamrongkit, Supasorn Suwajanakorn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While diffusion models excel at image generation, their denoising trajectories pass through corrupted intermediate states far from the image manifold. We ask whether generation can instead proceed through recognizable images throughout the entire trajectory. We introduce a generative framework built around a variation-reducing degradation process that contracts diverse images toward a shared prototype while preserving recognizable intermediate states. Because such manifold traversal lacks a simple analytical form, we construct the trajectories using generative-prior-based image morphing. We then distill the reverse process into a diversification operator that transforms the prototype into increasingly varied samples. To resolve the one-to-many ambiguity of the reverse process, we associate each trajectory with its initial noise latent and apply perturbations to improve robustness against accumulated errors. At inference, every step yields a recognizable image, allowing early termination and enabling a controllable speed--variation trade-off.
  </details>

- **[DoubleTake: Focused Verification of Visual Evidence for Long-Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17853)**  `arXiv:2611.17853`  
  _Kejun Xue, Ang Li, Jinghua Tang, Xincheng Wang, Yongbin Gao, Yanghua Xiao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In long-document question answering, a vision-language model can answer incorrectly even when the retrieved pages contain sufficient evidence. Rereading those pages with the original question gives the model little guidance about which fact to check. We introduce DoubleTake, a training-free framework for focused verification of visual evidence. An initial reading produces a candidate answer and a separate evidence record that guides retrieval for a second reading. A planner compares the two candidates and their supporting evidence to formulate a factual question. A verifier answers it from the source images without seeing either candidate or evidence record, and a reasoner uses this observation to resolve the original question. With a frozen Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct backbone, DoubleTake achieves the highest overall accuracy among the evaluated methods on each of four benchmarks. With the same pages and number of model calls, asking a focused factual question yields higher accuracy on all four benchmarks than asking the original question again. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PADSNet: Physics-Aware Dual-Stream Network with Biomechanical Priors for Laryngeal Lesion Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17852)**  `arXiv:2611.17852`  
  _Zhenzhen You, Weifeng Yu, Zhenghao Shi, Shuangli Du, Minghua Zhao, Yingcheng Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precise classification of laryngeal lesions is clinically crucial yet highly challenging, as inter-class visual differences are often subtle. While abnormalities in vocal fold vibratory dynamics provide complementary discriminative information, existing deep learning models predominantly rely on visual appearance and lack explicit mechanisms for modeling clinically meaningful vibratory dynamics. To address this, we propose PADSNet, a Physics-Aware Dual-Stream Network for laryngeal lesion classification that integrates visual representations with explicit biomechanical reasoning. Specifically, a Wavelet-Attention Enhancement Module (WAEM) is designed to bridge the spectral-semantic gap, enabling complementary learning of spatial texture and dynamic frequency representations. Meanwhile, a Knowledge Computing Module (KCM) extracts interpretable biomechanical indices from temporal glottal distance waveforms, including indicators of mobility, symmetry, glottal closure, and vibration stability. These descriptors are subsequently incorporated into a Physics-Aware Gating Module (PAGM) as biomechanical priors to adaptively recalibrate visual feature toward pathology-relevant vibratory patterns. Extensive experiments on a clinical dataset demonstrate that PADSNet achieves an accuracy of 0.8452, an F1-score of 0.8090, a sensitivity of 0.7953, and a specificity of 0.8866. These results indicate that explicitly integrating biomechanical priors with deep visual representations provides a more accurate, robust, and clinically interpretable framework for automated laryngeal lesion classification.
  </details>

- **[V-JEPA-LM: Video-Language Models That Read Dynamics Beyond Static, Text-Aligned Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17831)**  `arXiv:2611.17831`  
  _Keuntae Kim, Dongjun Kim, Yong Suk Choi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-model video encoders such as V-JEPA learn how scenes change (direction, speed, order, state) from video alone. To answer questions, they are joined to a language model trained on captions and question-answer pairs. But each caption or question names only a few attributes of a clip, so the model learns to retrieve those attributes rather than to read the clip, and the dynamics the encoder carries go unused: on the same items, a linear probe reads speed from frozen V-JEPA features at 87% while the VLM answers at chance. We show where dynamics lives in a frozen encoder: in a subspace shared across videos for predictive encoders such as V-JEPA, but not as a shared component of frame-wise, text-aligned encoders such as SigLIP. We then build counterfactual supervision that makes the language model read it: pixel transformations turn public videos into answer-balanced variants whose answers are set by the transformation and differ only in the change. Trained in a single stage on 4.53M public samples with 32 visual tokens per frame, where open video LLMs use several stages, 9M to 172M samples and 64 to 256 tokens per frame on average, the resulting V-JEPA-LM models (1B to 8B, Qwen3 and Llama) lead every size class in average score across eleven video benchmarks; our 4B model scores 72.1, against 61.1 for the best open 4B and 64.8 for the best open 8B model. A controlled encoder x supervision study shows that the encoder decides what can be read and the supervision what is read: at a matched budget, counterfactual supervision adds 4.5 points on average and 38 on the speed questions of natural benchmarks. Code and models:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Signal Scaffolding: Unlocking the Potential of Lightweight Models for Self-Supervised Monocular Depth Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17828)**  `arXiv:2611.17828`  
  _Zhijie Wang, Li Zhang, Yujie Chen, Shibiao Deng, Xin Gao, Houzhi Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the rising demand for edge devices, lightweight self-supervised monocular depth estimation methods have continued to evolve; however, existing methods generally suffer from poor accuracy. We find that the root cause may lies in the failure of conventional training regimes to fully exploit the inherent potential of lightweight models. To address this, we propose a novel three-phase training strategy for lightweight self-supervised monocular depth estimation, termed Signal Scaffolding (SS). Without introducing any additional dataset or parameters, we apply SS to ReForgeDepth, a lightweight network of our own design. Experiments on the KITTI and Cityscapes datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance in the accuracy-efficiency trade-off and running in real time on edge devices. These results suggest that lightweight models can be improved further within their existing capacity, and that the central question may be less about how parameters are allocated than about how to unlock this latent potential through training.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Endpoints: Bézier Paths for Vision Foundation Model Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17825)**  `arXiv:2611.17825`  
  _Chenzhi Zheng, Xi Xiao, Chih-Ting Liao, Qizhen Lan, Jingwen Lin, Tianyang Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frozen vision foundation models can be adapted by learning a few prompt tokens shared across all images. When prompts are updated as data shift or tasks change, the distribution of image features moves from one endpoint to another. Prior theory asks which endpoints prompts can reach; we ask whether they can travel between them along the shortest path. Because shared prompts compete with each image's own content for attention, they often cannot. We quantify this restriction as the extra transport cost that shared attention imposes over the shortest path. In an exactly solvable attention model, the reachable features form a cone; unrolling it into a plane gives the exact minimum action for every pair of endpoints and explicit optimal prompts on short arcs. The analysis reveals a sharp threshold in content normalization, which measures how much attention each image's own tokens absorb: for same-sign endpoints, at most two distinct levels allow prompts to follow the shortest path, whereas three or more force a strictly positive extra cost unless the endpoints differ only by rescaling. This gap persists with more prompts, small content perturbations, and aligned multihead outputs. On ViT-B/16 with VTAB-1k, a quadratic Bézier prompt path that minimizes feature action reduces held-out transport action relative to linear and cross-entropy-trained paths between the same endpoints and improves intermediate and online accuracy under unlabeled distribution shift, while cross-entropy-trained paths retain higher minimum accuracy when labeled calibration data are available. Reachable endpoints do not guarantee a reachable shortest path: how prompts move matters, not only where they end. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SceneAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Portable Game Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17821)**  `arXiv:2611.17821`  
  _Xingyi Li, Jiahao Yang, Yiqin Zhu, Jingwen Ye, Yang Sun, Weidong Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Moving a scene generator to a new game engine requires more than exporting its final output: planning, construction tools, and stored experience may also depend on the original backend. We present SceneAgent, a multi-agent framework for reusing the construction process and its experience across backends. A canonical SceneContext maintains the evolving world, while backend adapters isolate coordinate conventions, asset bindings, and export. Structured handoffs let fresh Builder sessions query this persistent state without inheriting the full construction transcript. Across generations, human-approved traces are distilled into asset-ID-free Layout skills and semantic Content skills that can be retrieved and recombined. We introduce GameSceneBench, comprising 69 indoor and outdoor prompts. Under a common asset and evaluation protocol, SceneAgent obtains human tie-adjusted preference scores of 78.2-89.4% against five baselines. Ablations show that reusable skills and the multi-agent protocol primarily improve prompt alignment and scene completeness, with additional runtime. Deployment in a second engine preserves the shared construction protocol and skill library while replacing backend integration components. Together, these results support separating persistent scene state, task-local reasoning, and reusable experience for portable scene construction.
  </details>

- **[Can Large Language Models Optimize Pretrained Neural Network Weights? A Multi-Phase Empirical Evaluation of Gradient-Free Editing in YOLO26 Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17789)**  `arXiv:2611.17789`  
  _Mohamed Abdallah Salem_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct weight editing offers a gradient-free alternative to fine-tuning pretrained detectors, but its generalization and the value of large language model (LLM) guidance remain uncertain. We evaluate five experimental phases using YOLO26 models and MS COCO, comparing deterministic scaling, random perturbations, differential evolution, CMA-ES, structured interventions, real local-LLM proposals, failure-aware guidance, and hybrid LLM-CMA-ES against a matched random-policy control. Optimization uses native Ultralytics mAP50-95 on 1,000 COCO Val2017 images; selected candidates are assessed on a disjoint, previously examined 1,500-image subset, with COCO API AP50-95 reported separately where available. Increasing the CMA-ES budget from 8 to 250 evaluations raised development gains from +0.11 to +0.28 percentage points, while selection changes remained negative (-0.03 to -0.12 points); the 250-evaluation result uses one seed. The hybrid LLM approach showed no statistically established advantage over random-policy guidance (p = 0.612, 0.369, and 0.694 at budgets 8, 25, and 50). Five-seed YOLO26m replication yielded a COCO API AP difference of +0.004 points (95% CI: -0.026 to +0.034). Of 225 archived LLM proposals, 53 satisfy the final strict schema; early tensor-level execution cannot be fully verified. Genuine LLM-mediated weight editing is feasible, but the tested approaches did not demonstrate consistent transfer beyond the development subset or a measurable LLM advantage.
  </details>

- **[Towards Seconds-level Video Representation: Quadratically Gated Feature Grid with Adaptive, Prediction-free Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17786)**  `arXiv:2611.17786`  
  _Tie-Yun Li, Hao-Ran Wang, Chao Wang, Zhiyong Yin, Yiming Xu, Zhenwei Cai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Implicit neural video representations are notoriously slow to build, which does not cross the basic practical threshold for most industrial applications. Here, we present Q-GAS, a coordinate-queryable video representation that couples multi-resolution explicit 3D feature grids with a compact SwiGLU-style quadratic-gating (QG) decoder and an error-aware, prediction-free sampler. The explicit grids avoid hash-collision coefficient sharing at the cost of modestly higher storage yet greatly boost the expressive capacity; the QG decoder introduces low-rank local second-order interactions to improve the nonlinear feature response. During fitting, a global error map periodically refreshes a high-error pool, and each batch mixes pool samples with uniform samples, which is free from the auxiliary model to predict difficult samples, thereby further escalating the efficiency. On UVG Beauty/Bosphorus/Jockey, Q-GAS fits and quantizes under 50 s on one RTX PRO 6000, decodes at over 370 FPS with mixed precision and about 146 FPS in FP32. In our PyTorch implementation, Q-GAS trains and quantizes 5.5-7.2 times faster than the evaluated Instant-NGP baseline and 19-28 times faster than RNeRV. Under a 90 s budget, it improves PSNR/MS-SSIM/LPIPS over the evaluated NeRV-family pipelines at comparable BPP over the measured overlap. We have shared our code atthis https URLfor readers' free use.
  </details>

- **[Learning Anisotropic Prediction Targets for Flow Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17767)**  `arXiv:2611.17767`  
  _Rayhan Zirvi, Hansheng Chen, Gordon Wetzstein, Leonidas Guibas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-based generative models are trained on a hand-chosen prediction target, such as the velocity, the noise, or the clean data. This choice strongly affects training and sample quality. We introduce Anisotropic Flow Models (AnisoFlow), which replaces a fixed prediction target with a fully learnable one. AnisoFlow learns to scale the noise component of the target anisotropically, assigning a separate nonnegative weight to each direction in a jointly learned basis. A closed-form map recovers the velocity from the network's prediction, leaving the architecture, flow-matching loss, and sampler unchanged. Since recovery is exact, training optimizes the target purely to make it easier for the network to predict. We theoretically analyze the resulting prediction error and predict that the preferred noise weight in each direction scales near-linearly with data variance, in agreement with the weights learned empirically in pixel space. AnisoFlow improves FID from 1.90 to 1.55 over the JiT baseline on ImageNet 256$\times$256, by learning only the prediction target, without representation alignment or architectural changes. In pixel-space text-to-image generation, AnisoFlow improves image quality and, through better-conditioned velocity recovery, largely removes patch-seam artifacts under reduced-precision inference.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Genotype Classification from 3D MRI in ADPKD via Density-Aware Point Sampling and E(3)-invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17758)**  `arXiv:2611.17758`  
  _Kwanghee Lee, Hyeongsub Lee, Youngwoo Kim, Hyunji Jeong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early detection of genetic kidney diseases, especially autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (ADPKD), is crucial because certain genotypes such as truncating PKD1 mutations cause rapid kidney growth and early progression to end-stage kidney disease. MRI-based genotype inference is a potential low-cost complement to genetic testing. However, although many deep learning methods have been developed for classifying various MR images, applying them to ADPKD remains challenging: (1) abdominal MR images are generally too large to be handled by deep learning models designed for smaller images like brain MRI, and (2) the amount of labeled data for ADPKD genetic mutations is insufficient to train deep learning models. In order to address these challenges, we propose E3PN, an efficient 3D MRI-based ADPKD genotype classification network that relies on point sampling and E(3)-invariance. We utilize a point representation of 3D MRI, which consumes less GPU memory than a voxel representation. For further memory efficiency, 3D points are sampled from a mixture distribution over the two kidneys in each image. Since E(3)-invariance has been shown to improve data efficiency, we also incorporate it into our model through coordinate canonicalization. On our ADPKD dataset, E3PN trains 163 times faster and uses 103x less GPU memory than a 3D ResNet, while also improving F1 from 0.165 to 0.226 over both baselines. These results establish a first benchmark for five-way ADPKD genotype classification from 3D MRI and show that point sampling and coordinate canonicalization yield two-orders-of-magnitude efficiency gains with a modest but consistent improvement in F1.
  </details>

- **[VACO: Vertex-Aligned Decoding for Dense Hand Contact Estimation from a Single Image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17755)**  `arXiv:2611.17755`  
  _Binhong Ye, Jin-Chuan Shi, Tao Liu, Xin Wang, Hao Chen, Chunhua Shen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense hand contact estimation from a single RGB image is a foundational primitive for dexterous manipulation and 3D interaction analysis. Direct predictors typically decode the entire per-vertex contact map from one shared query, leaving individual vertices ungrounded in the image and smearing contact across adjacent, non-touching surfaces. We present VACO, a vertex-aligned decoder in which each MANO vertex samples local image features at its projected location. A hand-reconstruction backbone, jointly trained for reconstruction and contact, predicts these locations at inference without an auxiliary reconstructor. Training uses noise-perturbed ground-truth projections and end-to-end feature adaptation to improve robustness and contact-sensitive features. A single VACO checkpoint sets a new state of the art on five benchmarks spanning hand-object, hand-human, hand-scene, and bimanual interactions. It improves F1 by +20.7 pp over the strongest prior direct predictor on Hi4D and generalizes to the held-out HOT3D and HOCap, with gains of +5.5 and +2.7 pp. The resulting contact predictions also improve downstream HOI reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[Stage-wise Lineage-aware Dropout for Sparse-View 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17752)**  `arXiv:2611.17752`  
  _Yan Xing, Zhi Chen, Fanglue Zhang, Junhong Zhao, Bing Xue, Mengjie Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse-view 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is prone to overfitting because only a limited number of images are available to constrain scene geometry. Dropout is one regularization strategy for alleviating this problem. We introduce the concept of a lineage family to describe a set of Gaussian primitives derived from the same founder through cloning or splitting operations. The founder Gaussians refer to all Gaussian primitives generated during initialization. Descendants within the same family often jointly contribute to the refinement of adjacent local regions. However, dropping individual Gaussians can be less effective due to the compensation effect from neighboring Gaussians for the dropped ones. To address this issue, we propose Stage-wise Lineage-aware Dropout(SLD), which applies lineage-family dropout during the structural regularization stage and switches to individual dropout during the later refinement stage. We further propose Lineage-Balanced Densification(LBD), which operates during densification by constructing an expanded candidate pool and reallocating the densification budget according to the mean gradient scores and existing descendant sizes of different families. Experiments on the LLFF, MipNeRF360, and Blender datasets show that the proposed framework achieves competitive performance in sparse-view reconstruction
  </details>

- **[HarmonyText: Harmonious Scene Text Editing via Reverse-to-Refined Pair Synthesis and Region-Decoupled Frequency Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17733)**  `arXiv:2611.17733`  
  _Ziqi Yi, Huiguo He, Guitao Xu, Peirong Zhang, Cheng Da, Xinyao Yu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural language-guided, mask-free scene text editing aims to modify text in real-world images using only textual instructions, yet remains challenged by unreliable pseudo-GT supervision, over-smoothed text generation, and the absence of GT-free evaluation. Existing methods often rely on imperfect pseudo-targets with background discrepancies, while standard MSE-based velocity regression tends to suppress high-frequency details, leading to blurred text boundaries and weakened local textures. To address these issues, we propose HarmonyText, a mask-free scene text editing framework with region- and frequency-aware supervision. HarmonyText introduces Region-Decoupling Optimization (RDO), which aligns edited text regions with pseudo-targets while anchoring preserved regions to the source image, and Cascaded High-Frequency Texture Guidance (CHFTG), which applies cascaded wavelet-domain constraints to preserve sharp boundaries and fine-grained textures of textual content. We further propose Reverse-to-Refined Pair Synthesis (RePair) to construct STE25K, a high-quality 25K paired dataset, and introduce a GT-free evaluation protocol with Color Distribution Divergence and a Harmony Assessment Model. Extensive experiments show that HarmonyText outperforms existing methods in text editing accuracy, background fidelity, color consistency, and perceptual harmony.
  </details>

- **[QCAP-VQA: Question-Conditioned Capped Prior-Mass Supervision for Medical Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17708)**  `arXiv:2611.17708`  
  _Yining Zhao, Catarina Moreira, Mukesh Prasad, Ali Braytee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical visual question answering models can answer correctly without attending to the anatomy that the question names. Automatic region priors allow spatial supervision without expert masks, but they are noisy, and it is unclear how strongly they should shape attention and how their effect should be verified. We present QCAP-VQA, which supervises a subset of cross-attention heads with a question-conditioned prior built from ranked segmentation proposals. Its capped prior-mass objective penalizes insufficient attention inside the prior only below a threshold. We evaluate attention against SLAKE structure boxes that play no role in training, separating attention on the named anatomy from how attention shifts between questions about the same image. QCAP-VQA matches the accuracy of a baseline without spatial supervision (84.26% vs. 84.23%), and every evaluated prior-supervised model places more attention on the annotated structures. Over a 64-fold range of regularization strength, the capped objective keeps prior mass between 0.67 and 0.77, whereas a pooled region-mass objective drives it to 0.97 and loses accuracy. The cost lies in question conditioning: as the constraint widens or strengthens, attention shifts agree less with the expert-defined contrast. Across 13 configurations, prior-referenced and expert-referenced evaluation rank the models in largely opposite order (Spearman correlation of -0.87), and the capped models it favors track the prior's own errors. Because clinical use of medical visual question answering requires answers grounded in the queried anatomy, supervision from automatic priors should be judged against independent anatomical references.
  </details>

- **[RaFL: An Empirical Study of Random Filter Losses for Image Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17707)**  `arXiv:2611.17707`  
  _Ruibo Wang, Fengbei Liu, Aleksei Tiulpin, Mert R. Sabuncu, Heejong Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pixel losses train image reconstruction networks toward a point statistic of the target distribution, often averaging away fine textures under uncertainty. Perceptual losses alleviate this issue using pretrained features, but those features are inherently domain-specific. We study a random filter loss (RaFL) that compares prediction and target through filters with random weights, avoiding both pretrained features and task-specific loss engineering. RaFL combines random convolutions, random Fourier features, and random wavelet filters, automatically balancing their contributions through gradient-norm matching, eliminating manual loss-weight tuning and grid search. We systematically investigate three design choices: filter family, weight resampling schedule, and weight initialization. Based on validation PSNR and stability across random seeds, we select the configuration that uses all three filter families, redraws filters every optimization step, and employs Gaussian initialization. We evaluate RaFL on three 2D and two 3D image reconstruction tasks. RaFL outperforms a pixel loss on three tasks and matches it on two, and demonstrates stable performance across random seeds on all five tasks. RaFL adds less than 2% to the backbone's forward FLOPs and no inference cost, requiring $182\times$ fewer loss FLOPs than a VGG19 perceptual loss. These findings suggest that RaFL is a simple, tuning-free, and domain-agnostic loss objective for 2D and 3D image reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[Open-World Scene Deocclusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17695)**  `arXiv:2611.17695`  
  _K.M.S.J. Kumarasinghe, Neil A. Dodgson, Fang-Lue Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering the complete appearance of occluded objects from a single image is a fundamental problem for scene understanding and image editing. Real-world scenes are challenging due to clutter, complex spatial occlusions, and diverse object categories. Existing methods often struggle when occlusion involves background surfaces or objects that are not explicitly detected, and fail to resolve the cyclic occlusion dependencies that frequently occur in natural scenes. We introduce a training-free, open-world scene deocclusion framework that recovers the full amodal appearance of detected or user-selected objects from a single RGB image, decomposing the scene into depth-ordered RGBA layers that are geometrically plausible, semantically faithful, and visually consistent with the visible scene. Extensive experiments show that our approach improves scene-level deocclusion, producing coherent and realistic reconstructions in real-world settings. Code will be available on the project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Provable Cross-Modal Transfer of Hierarchical Concept Structure in CLIP Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17671)**  `arXiv:2611.17671`  
  _Nghia Nguyen, Tianjiao Ding, Lachlan Ewen MacDonald, René Vidal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contrastive vision-language models such as CLIP are trained to align images and text, not to represent taxonomies. Yet empirical studies show that CLIP embeddings exhibit hierarchical structure aligned with the hierarchy of semantic concepts. A theoretical characterization of why such hierarchy emerges from a non-hierarchical objective remains open. In this paper, we formalize this phenomenon as Hierarchical Angular Separation (HAS), a geometric property whereby each non-leaf concept embedding is more similar to its descendants than to off-branch concepts. Empirically, we show that HAS holds approximately in raw images and randomly initialized CLIP image embeddings, while in text embeddings it holds approximately only after contrastive learning. To theoretically explain this asymmetry, we analyze an unconstrained features model in which the image embeddings are fixed and only the text embeddings are trained. At the global minimizer, the text-side HAS violation percentage at each node is at most the image-side HAS violation and near-violation percentage. The key mechanism is the hierarchical co-occurrence structure of image-text pairs, which biases text embeddings toward root-centered image prototypes. For a linear contrastive loss, we derive a closed-form solution that makes this mechanism explicit. We extend the analysis to the InfoNCE loss and establish analogous guarantees under sufficient regularization. Synthetic experiments validate the theory and show that HAS is transferred even beyond the regimes covered by our analysis.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free Acceleration of Multi-View 3D Transformers via Regime-Aware Attention Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17629)**  `arXiv:2611.17629`  
  _Muhammed Izzet Sezer, Sevval Belkis Dikkaya_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-view 3D transformers such as VGGT reconstruct a scene from a set of unposed images in a single forward pass, predicting camera poses, depth and 3D points, but the cost of their global attention grows quadratically with the num- ber of images. Accelerators borrowed from large language models assume that each token attends to only a few oth- ers; on these models they lose accuracy or gain little speed. We study the global attention of three multi-view transform- ers on seven datasets and find that every layer plays one of three roles: it averages over the whole scene, matches points across many images, or anchors on the first image, whose coordinates the outputs use. Building on this anal- ysis, we propose Regime-AWare ATTention Approximation (RAWATTA), a training-free method that runs attention in 8- bit precision and keeps 16-bit precision only in the layers where a worst-case calibration check requires it. Its fast set- ting, RAWATTA-Fast, also approximates attention in a few averaging and anchoring layers, while matching layers and the first image keep all their keys. On a 12 GB GPU at 50 im- ages, RAWATTA runs VGGT faster than the released code, mostly through our CUDA engine, and loses at most about one point of pose accuracy (AUC@3) on any dataset; RAWATTA-Fast is slightly faster and less accurate. Both fit 200 images, where the released code runs out of memory at 100.
  </details>

- **[Where to Predict in Retinal OCT? Anatomy-Guided Target Selection for I-JEPA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17586)**  `arXiv:2611.17586`  
  _Yunchu Feng, Wai Tak Lau, Kaveri A. Thakoor_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised learning aims to learn meaningful representations from unlabeled data, potentially allowing models to capture subtle anatomical variations across patients and reduce the amount of labeled data needed. Image-based Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (I-JEPA) learns by predicting target-region representations from visible context rather than reconstructing pixels. However, its uniform target placement does not account for the anatomical structure of retinal OCT, where retinal tissue occupies only part of each B-scan. We investigate anatomy-guided target selection using image intensity and predicted retinal segmentation while retaining the I-JEPA architecture and latent prediction objective. We compare anatomy-guided rectangular targets, anatomy-shaped targets and coverage-constrained placement with uniform masking, and evaluate the learned representations using linear probing for glaucoma classification on FairVision. We found that rectangular targets guided by image intensity achieved the highest linear-probe AUC in our comparisons. We show that anatomically meaningful target selection is promising for retinal OCT representation learning, while greater anatomical specificity did not consistently improve performance.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Visual Reasoning in Text-to-Image Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17585)**  `arXiv:2611.17585`  
  _Shiye Su, Xiaohan Wang, Ludwig Schmidt, Serena Yeung-Levy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models are a core component of modern text-to-image systems, serving as the objective for inference-time selection and RL-based post-training. A key prerequisite is visual understanding: to judge prompt adherence and ground aesthetic preferences, a reward model must bind attributes, resolve relations, and count reliably. We find that widely-used reward models perform poorly on targeted visual reasoning evaluations. Even when starting from strong vision-language backbones, preference fine-tuning erodes these capabilities, and continual-learning baselines fail to mitigate a steep tradeoff between visual reasoning and preference accuracy. We propose a data-centric intervention that injects visual reasoning supervision into preference learning by flipping the pairing axis. To complement conventional human preference data (paired images under a shared prompt), we convert existing visual reasoning datasets into preference pairs that share the same image but differ in prompt, and train on the mixture. This yields a Pareto improvement in both visual understanding and preference prediction. Across inference-time best-of-n selection and RL post-training, the resulting reward model improves text-to-image generation on challenging compositional prompts (GenEval) by an average of 4.0% and 5.5% respectively, outperforming HPSv3 which was trained on 6x more preference pairs.
  </details>

- **[Adversarial Tracking: Attacks on Image Sensors for Object Tracking in Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17505)**  `arXiv:2611.17505`  
  _Han Wu, Alex Chan, Ekin Can Erkus, Tousif Rahman, Rishad Shafik_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object tracking systems estimate the motion of surrounding vehicles by linking detected objects across video frames. In particular, Tracking-By-Detection (TBD) systems combine object detectors with motion prediction and data association to maintain object identities over time. However, neural network-based detectors are vulnerable to adversarial attacks that perturb input images, and their impact on downstream trajectory estimation is not fully characterized by detection accuracy alone. Therefore, this paper evaluates image-specific and image-agnostic attacks on YOLOv3 and YOLOv4 detectors paired with SORT, Deep SORT, Strong SORT, and OC-SORT tracking systems. The evaluation uses CARLA simulator and measures tracking quality through Multiple Object Tracking Accuracy (MOTA), Higher Order Tracking Accuracy (HOTA), and Identification F1 (IDF1). Under image-specific attacks, HOTA for YOLOv3-based trackers declines from 53.45-56.19% to 14.85-17.03%, while MOTA reaches -772.60% in the worst case. YOLOv4-based trackers exhibit greater robustness, while HOTA still declines to 25.06-26.86%. In addition, image-agnostic attacks can reduce the tracking accuracy using a single Universal Adversarial Perturbation (UAP), without generating a separate perturbation for individual frames.
  </details>

- **[Real-Time Diffusion Relighting as a Neural Sensor: Closing the Low-Light Domain Gap for Robotic Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17438)**  `arXiv:2611.17438`  
  _Shengjie Xu, Alexander Gao, Anshul Rai, Dae Yeol Lee, Guan-Ming Su, Ming C. Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-light conditions severely degrade robotic perception: object detectors trained on daylight imagery are prone to failure under dark and adverse weather conditions that fall outside of their training distribution. Furthermore, from an efficiency standpoint, existing Low-Light Image Enhancement (LLIE) methods are designed for offline processing and are therefore impractical to integrate into live control loops. To address these issues, we propose to embed a Generative Neural Sensor within the control loop that treats illumination as a generative prior rather than a signal processing problem, enabling task-relevant semantic recovery. We further propose an Real-Time Perceptual Architecture that decouples high-latency semantic recovery from low-latency robotic control. Rather than blocking the control loop, our system runs a large-scale image-to-image diffusion model in a background thread, and is activated only when a drop in detection confidence signals a perceptual uncertainty event, significantly reducing computational overhead. Our method sets a new state-of-the-art performance on the ExDark low-light benchmark with 87.1% mAP, and under adverse weather conditions, significantly boosts detection rates over competing methods. On a navigation task in a closed-loop autonomous driving simulator (CARLA), we achieve a 100% lap completion rate, establishing a simulation-based proof-of-concept for integrating high-latency generative models with standard robotic control autonomy stacks.
  </details>

- **[ParseBench-2: Severity-Aware Evaluation of Document Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17400)**  `arXiv:2611.17400`  
  _Pierre-Loïc Doulcet, Boyang Zhang, Sebastián G. Acosta, Sacha Bron, Daniel B. Ospina, Antonio Jimeno Yepes, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Document-parsing metrics can give high scores to outputs that change important facts and penalize changes that preserve meaning. People can often judge these consequences more easily than they can design a metric to measure them. We introduce a method for developing parsing metrics from severity policies that specify how each error type should affect the score. We express these policies through test scenarios that specify how a metric should behave. We then optimize the metric implementations against these fixed expectations, using coding agents to refine comparison rules, error detectors and weights under human review. This makes stated human priorities an explicit objective of metric design. We apply this method in ParseBench-2, a comprehensive document-parsing benchmark that combines broad capability coverage with metrics calibrated to error severity. It covers 4,317 pages across six dimensions of parsing quality: text, formatting, tables, charts, visual grounding, and forms. We use detailed annotations and targeted difficult cases to check for changed facts, broken structural relationships, and missing content. We take the lower of a parsing score that measures content recovery and a severity cap that penalizes detected errors, so recovering most of a page cannot hide a serious error. Across six severity test suites, our metrics more closely match the expert judgments expressed by the scenario targets than existing metrics and penalize at most 3.4% of the tested benign variations. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation spanning frontier vision-language models, specialized document parsers and open-weight systems, comparing their overall rankings, performance across parsing capabilities and tradeoffs between accuracy and cost.
  </details>

- **[Gravity and Flight: Learning to Navigate Fine-Grained Remote Sensing Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16408)**  `arXiv:2611.16408`  
  _Yuheng Zong, Minghua Wang, Xin Zhao, Xiaobin Liu, Yongjian Deng, Chanho Eom_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models are increasingly capable, yet their reasoning in complex remote sensing scenes can remain coarse-grained and insufficiently grounded in task-relevant visual evidence. We propose Gravity and Flight (G&F), a framework for scene specialization with limited supervision that separates visual and reasoning verification while coupling policy updates. It combines Evidence-of-Thought supervised fine-tuning (EoT-SFT) and Relational Transfer Policy Optimization (RTPO). EoT-SFT teaches fine-grained observation and faithful reasoning through description, analysis, and supported conclusions. RTPO independently samples responses to scene-matched image pairs with fine-grained visual differences under a shared task. Its rubric-based verification combines Gravity, which checks visual fidelity and description coverage, and Flight, which takes descriptions as premises to assess evidence use and relations between paired judgments. Their scores and task correctness yield a shared pair-level advantage that couples policy updates from both responses. Using 1,008 demonstrations and 500 training pairs for harbor specialization, G&F achieves a mean score of 69.12 across six evaluation sets, exceeding the backbone and supervised initialization by 9.76 and 4.56 points. Matched controls show gains over single-image and joint-input RL, and removing either verification view reduces performance. Applying G&F to urban specialization also improves on the base model and supervised initialization across three evaluation subsets. Project details and updates are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CARE-Drive: Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15360)**  `arXiv:2611.15360`  
  _Xiao Ma, Yijun Chen, Zhiquan Hu, Jicheng Zhao, Ran Ji, Yuming Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models bring multimodal understanding and world knowledge to end-to-end autonomous driving. To further improve the quality of predicted trajectories, existing studies introduce candidate refinement, residual denoising, and token correction. However, these methods typically focus on unified refinement of the entire trajectory, paying limited attention to the distinct physical quantities and their interactions. We further investigate physical specialization and coordination in trajectory refinement, centering on position and heading for simpler and more intuitive correction modeling. To this end, we propose CARE-Drive (Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories), which decomposes trajectory refinement into waypoint residual correction and position-conditioned heading generation. Through imitation learning and a future-token auxiliary task, the backbone provides predictive representations and a base trajectory. The position expert predicts bounded metric waypoint residuals, while the heading expert reconstructs vehicle orientation from adjacent angular increments conditioned on the corrected path. With the backbone frozen, the two experts are optimized alternately through supervised initialization and reinforcement fine-tuning. This design simplifies refinement through clear physical specialization while preserving conditional coupling during prediction and optimization. On NAVSIM v1, CARE-Drive achieves 88.8 PDMS, improving by 2.4 points over the unrefined base trajectories generated by the same backbone. Ablation studies further support the effectiveness of specialized experts and staged training.
  </details>

- **[Before Words, Beyond Speech: Evaluating Nonverbal Social Reasoning in Early Childhood](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13213)**  `arXiv:2611.13213`  
  _Marie Amale Huynh, Laura Bravo-Sánchez, Lauren Klein, Nick Haber, Philip Fisher, Serena Yeung-Levy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Children's earliest years are a period of intense social learning, during which meaning is built through gaze, gesture, and physical contact. Yet, this critical aspect of social interaction remains under-evaluated in multimodal video understanding, as existing benchmarks prioritize the conversation-driven interactions of adults. We address this gap with \bench ($\textbf{N}$aturalistic $\textbf{E}$arly-childhood $\textbf{S}$ocial in$\textbf{T}$eractions), the first child-centric benchmark for nonverbal social reasoning. \bench comprises \totalvids manually annotated clips ($\sim$12s) curated for diversity across naturalistic settings, cultural contexts, and developmental stages. Beyond a lack of data, targeted model improvement in this domain is hindered by coarse evaluation schemas that conflate basic perception with higher-order reasoning. To resolve this, \bench introduces a compositional schema that builds atomic interaction cues into progressive levels of social reasoning: contextual, behavioral, interpersonal, and open-ended descriptions. Extensive analysis of state-of-the-art Vision-Language Models (VLMs) reveals a stark performance collapse: despite strong contextual recognition, models exhibit substantial gaps in decoding precise behavioral and interpersonal cues. We trace these failures to specific biases, including poor consequence grounding and temporal sensitivity. Finally, we ground \bench in real-world applications by evaluating VLMs using domain adaptation strategies. Ultimately, \bench provides a rigorous testbed for advancing socially grounded artificial intelligence in early childhood. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Decoupling Direction and Magnitude: Language-Steered Flow Matching for Super-Resolution in the Dark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10485)**  `arXiv:2611.10485`  
  _Jiaxin Gao, Ziyu Yue, Yaohua Liu, Danchen Cui, Zhihui Zhao, Zhixun Su_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image super-resolution in the dark is fundamentally challenged by extreme spatial heterogeneity: severely underexposed and noisy regions demand aggressive generative enhancement to reconstruct missing details, while relatively well-exposed areas require conservative updates to prevent hallucinated textures. Standard diffusion and flow-matching models, however, rely on globally uniform integration trajectories, making them sub-optimal for such spatially varying degradations. In this paper, we propose DeLang-SR (Degradation-Language steered Super-Resolution), a one-step flow-matching framework that explicitly decouples the restoration direction and magnitude. Rather than treating low-light corruption as arbitrary latent features, DeLang-SR translates measurable physical statistics into structured degradation language, harnessing the semantic prior of pretrained text-to-image models. The degradation-language intent, complemented by image-specific visual tokens, steers the velocity field (direction) toward restoration-relevant manifolds. Simultaneously, a continuous spatial update-strength field acts as a locally varying Euler step-size field (magnitude), applying stronger integration steps in heavily degraded shadows while enforcing conservative updates in reliable regions. Experiments on paired and real dark images, together with ablations and diagnostic analyses, show that DeLang-SR provides an effective and interpretable one-step route to super-resolution in the dark. Code and pretrained models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Anterior Cruciate Ligament Risk of Injury Using a Graph Convolutional Autoencoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09724)**  `arXiv:2611.09724`  
  _Athanasios Babouras, Paul A. Martineau, Thomas Fevens_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current solutions for assessing the risk of anterior cruciate ligament (ACL) injury typically rely on expensive motion-capture labs that require substantial resource investment or subjective evaluations of specific athletic stress tests by experts. The Drop Vertical Jump (DVJ) and the Single Leg Hop (SLH) are two examples of such tests typically used to track progress in ACL rehabilitation. In this paper, we develop an unsupervised learning model that runs entirely on a smartphone for two tasks, based on an autoencoder architecture with multiple layers of graph convolutional networks, which utilizes pose estimation data to determine a person's injury risk status. Our model accurately predicts athletes at high risk of injury for both athletic stress tests, with average sensitivities and specificities of 78% and 78% for the DVJ, and 75% and 74% for the SLH. Our findings support the development of a widely accessible smartphone solution for real-time screening of ACL injury risk.
  </details>

- **[TAQ-MOTR: Tracking-Aware Detection Query Formation for End-to-End Multiple Object Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05205)**  `arXiv:2611.05205`  
  _Hanyang Liu, Hong Zhang, Ding Yuan, Yang Han, Yifan Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tracking-by-propagation (TBP) methods perform end-to-end multi-object tracking by propagating track queries across frames, jointly handling detection and identity association without heuristic post-processing while naturally modeling temporal information. However, as detection and association are tightly coupled, detection queries in TBP are expected to discover currently untracked objects and provide complementary current-frame evidence for challenging tracks whose queries and identities are retained. Existing methods largely inherit detection-query formation from single-frame detection and construct such queries before the decoder with little awareness of trajectory states. Consequently, currently untracked objects may be missed, while challenging tracked objects with reduced visibility under occlusion may lack complementary evidence, causing association failures or duplicate responses. To address these issues, we propose TAQ-MOTR, a tracking-aware detection query formation framework that employs a pre-decoder Hybrid Query Generator (HQG) to fuse current-frame image features, track memory, and learnable memory. By conditioning detection queries on trajectory states, TAQ-MOTR adapts them to \rev{the discovery of currently untracked objects and tracked-object complementation We further introduce a training-only auxiliary detection branch whose one-to-one label assignment is decoupled from that of the main tracking branch, providing additional detection supervision to strengthen the shared HQG and decoder for the discovery of currently untracked objects. TAQ-MOTR improves performance on DanceTrack, SportsMOT, and BFT, achieving 72.0 HOTA, 63.1 AssA, and 76.6 IDF1 on the DanceTrack test set. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Mamba3R: Fixed-Capacity Selective State Memory for Long-Sequence 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04221)**  `arXiv:2611.04221`  
  _Zhenxiang Xiong, Tianchen Deng, Nailin Wang, Lei Zhao, Guole Shen, Weidong Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming 3D reconstruction must maintain geometric quality over long inputs within a fixed GPU budget. A pretrained recurrent model provides bounded state, but freezing its backbone also fixes its learned state-observation interactions. We introduce Mamba3R, a jointly designed state-memory adapter that provides a trainable adaptation path without updating the backbone or task heads. A selective state-space mixer jointly transforms native state features and a fixed-capacity auxiliary memory, explicit read and write paths carry information across frames, and a decomposed feedback path regulates what the added memory feeds back. Only 1.28 million adapter parameters are trained. Under a shared periodic-reset protocol, it lowers trajectory error and improves accumulated surface and depth accuracy over recurrent baselines on public indoor and outdoor datasets and robot-recorded traversals, gaining up to 40 percent on one metric. Matched comparisons support both the choice of the mixing operator and the utility of carrying the auxiliary memory across frames. Allocated memory remains unchanged and throughput moves by at most 1.4 percent from one to two thousand frames. Replaying its recorded traversals offline, the platform's RTX 4060 averages 11.7 frames per second within 5.1 GiB with the same checkpoint, a mean throughput covering the 10 Hz sensor rate.
  </details>

- **[Real-VAS: a Real-World Video Amodal Segmentation Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00430)**  `arXiv:2611.00430`  
  _Finlay G. C. Hudson, William A. P. Smith_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Amodal video object segmentation is fundamentally limited by the absence of datasets that combine real-world complexity with precise ground-truth annotations. To address this, we present Real Video Amodal Segmentation (Real-VAS), a new large-scale, zero-shot evaluation dataset built from composited real-image video. We introduce a novel data generation pipeline that enables the creation of programmatically consistent amodal ground truth for real-world footage, in which the object mask, occlusion boundary, and composited image are all derived from a single unoccluded source, without relying on human estimation or expensive 3D reconstruction. Our dataset is structured into two challenging scenarios: dynamic Occlusion, created by compositing two clips of moving objects, and a unique Container category featuring complex, physically constrained interactions. These container scenarios--On Surface Containment, Articulated Containment, and Mobile Containment--allow us to generate precise ground truth by simulating an object's motion based on its container's tracked transformation. As a result, Real-VAS provides a diverse and challenging benchmark for evaluating amodal segmentation models on realistic video with the precision of synthetic data. The dataset and generation code are publicly available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[TAP-Out: Tracking Any Point in 360° via Outpainting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00406)**  `arXiv:2611.00406`  
  _Finlay G. C. Hudson, James A. D. Gardner, William A. P. Smith_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tracking points that leave the camera's field of view (FoV) requires reasoning about unobserved scene dynamics, a fundamentally different problem from tracking visible correspondences. We present TAP-Out, an offline method that makes this out-of-view (OOV) reasoning explicit by leveraging generative video outpainting as a world-model prior. Given a narrow-FoV input video, TAP-Out outpaints a complete 360° equirectangular video, locates target objects within the panoramic scene via video object segmentation, and synthesises object-centred perspective crops on which standard in-frame trackers operate. This decoupled design composes off-the-shelf foundation models without requiring task-specific architectural modifications. On the TAPVid360 benchmark, TAP-Out achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving OOV tracking accuracy by approximately 3x over the best prior method. We demonstrate its flexibility on out-of-frame object segmentation and detection and we provide detailed diagnostic analyses across motion regimes, static versus dynamic content, and hallucination quality, characterising when and why the approach succeeds or fails. Code is publicly available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Zoom Where the Answer Looks: Cross-Resolution Self-Distillation for MLLMs under Visual-Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32664)**  `arXiv:2610.32664`  
  _Zhensheng Hu, Guanglong Xu, Guanqiang Hu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Under the small per-view visual-token budgets of efficient deployment, the fine text of documents, charts and infographics becomes illegible to multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Zooming in recovers it, but the region must be chosen before the model answers. Existing methods decode an answer or a box first, at the cost of generation, or read the attention available before answering, which localizes the evidence only in top layers whose depth varies with model size. Learned region proposers instead distill the attention of the model's answers, but on the same budgeted view. Yet this attention is precise only on a view the model can read: on Qwen3-VL-4B, a window around its peak covers 83% of the evidence on a high-resolution view but 54% on the budgeted view. Since answers and high-resolution views are available during training, we introduce LookZoom, a zooming method that trains a dedicated look token by cross-resolution self-distillation of the answer's attention: with the MLLM frozen, the token learns to attend on the budgeted view, before any answer, where the model's own correct answers attend on a high-resolution view. The region around its attention peak is the window to zoom into, and reinforcement learning then trains the MLLM to answer from both views. With 98 visual tokens per view on five text-rich benchmarks, LookZoom surpasses attention-guided zooming by 20-24 points and grounding-based zooming and learned crop routing by 12-15 points on Qwen3-VL at three scales, and it outperforms learned region proposers at every budget from 98 to 392 tokens. On Qwen3-VL, the look token alone, with the MLLM frozen, already surpasses every baseline that leaves the language model unchanged, and the recipe carries over to InternVL3.5 and Qwen2.5-VL.
  </details>

- **[Brain2Vec: A Self-Supervised 3D Foundation Model for Structural Brain MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28411)**  `arXiv:2610.28411`  
  _Spiros Maggioros, Guray Erus, Gareth Harman, George Aidinis, Alexander Getka, Aristeidis Sotiras, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models have become essential tools in medical imaging, yet training them end-to-end for diverse tasks remains challenging due to limited labeled data and computational constraints. To address this, we introduce Brain2Vec, a foundation model pre-trained on 74,425 T1-weighted Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) scans pooled from multiple studies. Brain2Vec can serve both as a weight initialization backbone for fine-tuning downstream tasks and as a standalone feature extractor. We evaluated Brain2Vec in three tasks: downstream predictive performance, convergence speed, and embedding quality. Using Brain2Vec's pretrained weights as initialization and fine-tuning on labeled data for a brain age prediction task, results demonstrate improved final accuracy and faster convergence compared to random initialization. Additionally, its frozen embeddings outperform anatomically defined volumetric features on four of five downstream classification tasks and perform competitively with a state-of-the-art foundation model pre-trained on substantially more data. On the independent BrainFMBench benchmark, Brain2Vec ranked first among six pretrained brain MRI foundation models on 9 of 15 sex, age, and BMI prediction tasks across five cohorts. Lastly, we show that Brain2Vec encodes information primarily from a spatially localized and neuroanatomically meaningful set of brain regions, and that manipulating a subset of latent features produces monotonic, region-specific effects on the reconstructed anatomy. Brain2Vec code and trained models are available as an open-source package onthis https URL. Users can apply Brain2Vec locally by installing the package, or directly through the NiChart cloud platformthis https URL, where Brain2Vec features can be derived without requiring local installation or specialized infrastructure.
  </details>

- **[Synthetic Image Oversampling for Rare-Class Representation Learning in Deep Facial Phenotyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23730)**  `arXiv:2610.23730`  
  _Miles Liao, Zhanliang Wang, Hongzhuo Chen, Kai Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent deep facial phenotyping methods can support the recognition of genetic syndromes, but patient images are often scarce and unevenly distributed across disorders. Synthetic facial images offer a way to supplement the training data without collecting additional patient photographs. Their usefulness, however, depends on whether they improve recognition of real patients: generated images may introduce artifacts or repeat features already represented in the training set. It is therefore necessary to examine both how much synthetic augmentation to use and which disorders benefit. We investigate disease-specific synthetic oversampling using images from the Phenotypic Disease Image Database to augment a subset of the GestaltMatcher Database. We compare increasing augmentation levels in an image-based model, GestaltMatcher-Arc, and a multimodal model, GestaltMML, while keeping the real training and patient-disjoint evaluation partitions fixed. Repeated training runs assess overall and disease-specific accuracy, and an exploratory embedding analysis examines changes in class compactness and separation. Augmentation improves mean recognition accuracy in both training setups, although gains remain modest in GestaltMatcher-Arc and continue across the tested levels in GestaltMML. These aggregate improvements are accompanied by uneven disease-level responses, including reduced accuracy for some disorders. In the embedding analysis, disorders with fewer real training images tend to gain more class separation, but geometric changes do not consistently track classification performance. Together, the findings support synthetic oversampling for limited-data facial phenotyping and emphasize the need to assess its amount and benefit for individual disorders within each training setup.
  </details>

- **[Mind the RefGAP: Correcting Reference Attention in Diffusion-Based Visual Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35708)**  `arXiv:2609.35708`  
  _Yanan Wang, Shengcai Liao, Guangyi Liu, Xiaodan Liang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference-guided diffusion editors struggle to faithfully reproduce user-provided references. We observe that many diffusion editors allocate limited attention to the reference.
