For example, in LoomVideo, edit-region queries assign less than 1% of their attention mass to the reference. We introduce RefGAP, a training-free correction that determines logit-offset magnitudes online at each layer from the reference-attention mass measured during the forward pass. Positive offsets to reference logits strengthen reference usage by edit-region queries, while negative offsets for keep-region queries limit reference-induced changes outside the edit. Two global coefficients control the correction; they are selected once on validation data from four development diffusion editors and held fixed. Across seven diffusion-based image/video editors, RefGAP improves identity fidelity in head swapping and face swapping, where identity takes priority; on some editors this raises pose error, which delaying the correction reduces. RefGAP achieves a fidelity-preservation trade-off comparable to separately tuned constant edit-side biases, without per-approach strength sweeps. Additional experiments on virtual try-on and background replacement evaluate transfer beyond identity editing.
  </details>

- **[The Alignment Illusion in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30210)**  `arXiv:2609.30210`  
  _Hong-Han Wang, Yuntao Wang, Hu Ding_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Layer-wise visual-text similarity in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) is widely interpreted as evidence that the language model progressively integrates visual content into a shared representation space. This reading rests on the assumption that scalar alignment scores reflect content-level cross-modal interaction. To test this assumption, we apply controlled interventions to the visual stream. Across 13 MLLMs from five families spanning 0.5B to 72B parameters, replacing projector-output visual tokens with Gaussian noise sharply reduces task accuracy, yet four standard scalar measures (CKA, SVCCA, MIR, and the leading principal-angle cosine) fail to consistently separate the corrupted stream from the original. We call this failure the alignment illusion and trace it to the shared language-model pathway: anisotropic MLP down-projections pull visual and text tokens toward common output directions, producing weight-induced alignment. We introduce the principal-angle gap (PA gap), defined as the difference between the top two principal-angle cosines, to distinguish leading-direction dominance from the broader overlap observed with structured visual content. Under graded visual corruption, the PA gap tracks task accuracy more consistently than the scalar scores we consider; under a structured but irrelevant image, it further exposes regimes in which internal geometry and task accuracy come apart. Internal visual-text alignment in MLLMs is therefore best read as a geometric diagnostic of the visual stream inside the language model rather than a direct proxy for content-level cross-modal interaction, and is most informative when calibrated by controlled task evidence.
  </details>

- **[UltraBench 2: Towards Robust Evaluation of Vision Foundation Models on Ultrasound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28610)**  `arXiv:2609.28610`  
  _Ashwath Radhachandran, Adam Tupper, Christian Gagné, William Speier_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarking is an increasingly critical part of research in machine learning and the domains where it is applied, including healthcare. Yet, despite the steady development of new ultrasound foundation models in recent years, the development of well-designed benchmarks to evaluate them has lagged behind. This deficiency has led to fragmented and inconsistent evaluations of competing models, making it difficult to measure progress. To address this issue, we introduce UltraBench 2, a comprehensive benchmark with wide anatomical and task coverage, and a focus on standardization, reproducibility, and ease-of-use. Using this benchmark, we compare existing vision foundation models for ultrasound image analysis. Our analyses demonstrate that ultrasound-specific pretraining still leads on classification, but that state-of-the-art general-purpose models have drawn level on segmentation.
  </details>

- **[AnaDiffusion: Anatomically Compositional Latent Diffusion for Controllable 3D Brain MRI Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23014)**  `arXiv:2608.23014`  
  _Huiwen Han, Lulin Liu, Bangya Liu, Yuanhao Cai, Nuo Chen, Xiaoqing Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D brain MRI generation has made significant advances in medical imaging, simulation, and controllable anatomical analysis. However, existing generative models typically synthesize 3D volumes monolithically, often overlooking regional anatomical structures and limiting local controllability. To address these limitations, we introduce AnaDiffusion, an anatomically compositional latent diffusion framework that factorizes the generation process into distinct, anatomically meaningful regions, followed by part-to-whole assembly and global refinement. Our approach first trains part diffusion models to capture local structural priors. We then inject an assembled anatomical composite of the parts into the whole-brain latent representation and continue denoising. This mechanism enables the model to resolve global context while preserving the injected anatomy. AnaDiffusion thus produces explicit part assets and a globally coherent volume, enabling controllable part editing without subject-specific dense segmentation maps at inference. On a subject-disjoint ADNI test split, AnaDiffusion attains the lowest mean Fréchet distance (FID) in every evaluated region. In localized editing, it achieves substantially higher target transfer (paired MS-SSIM) than a segmentation-conditioned baseline, with off-target preservation close to the baseline's.
  </details>

- **[Projection Pursuit CPCANet for Domain Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22117)**  `arXiv:2607.22117`  
  _Yu-Hsi Chen, Abd-Krim Seghouane_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Domain Generalization (DG) aims to learn representations robust to distribution shifts. Recent geometric alignment methods, such as CPCANet, extract domain-invariant structures through batch-wise Common Principal Component Analysis (CPCA). However, CPCANet suffers from rank-deficient covariance estimation due to the small-sample-size issue in mini-batch training. To address this limitation, we propose Projection Pursuit CPCANet (PP-CPCANet), a covariance-free framework that learns a global orthogonal basis on the Stiefel manifold and jointly optimizes it with network parameters via the Cayley transform. We further introduce a symmetry-breaking detached-median PP dispersion objective to extract common principal components (CPCs) with dense and robust optimization signals. Experiments on four DG benchmarks show that PP-CPCANet achieves SOTA performance while maintaining stable training.
  </details>

- **[The Second LoViF 2026 Challenge on Real-World All-in-One Image Restoration: Methods and Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21118)**  `arXiv:2607.21118`  
  _Xiang Chen, Hao Li, Jiangxin Dong, Jinshan Pan, Xin Li, Hongbo Ding, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a review of the second LoViF Challenge on Real-World All-in-One Image Restoration. The challenge aims to advance unified image restoration under diverse real-world degradation conditions, including blur, low-light, haze, rain, and snow. It provides a common benchmark for evaluating the restoration accuracy, robustness, and generalization capability of models across multiple degradation categories within a unified framework. The competition attracted 158 registered participants, and 20 teams were included in the final ranking after their submitted results were successfully reproduced and verified. This report provides a comprehensive analysis of the submitted solutions and corresponding results, highlighting recent advances in real-world all-in-one image restoration. The summarized methods and empirical findings reveal effective design strategies and establish an updated benchmark for future research in real-world low-level vision.
  </details>

- **[RFMSR: Residual Flow Matching for Image Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12753)**  `arXiv:2607.12753`  
  _Shuwei Huang, Tianyao Luo, Jicheng Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative image super-resolution (ISR) methods typically sample from a standard Gaussian prior and transport the sample to the data distribution, where the low-quality (LQ) input serves only as an external condition. However, the LQ image already carries rich structural information about the target high-quality (HQ) image, which makes a distribution approximating the LQ image a more suitable starting point, an idea that has been validated by a series of diffusion-based restoration methods. In contrast, recent flow-matching-based ISR methods still adopt the standard Gaussian prior and thereby discard such structural priors; moreover, existing single-step acceleration techniques, such as distillation and consistency training, either depend on an additional teacher or sacrifice the model's multi-step inference capability. In this paper, we propose Residual Flow Matching for Image Super-Resolution (RFMSR), which defines the source distribution as a Gaussian centered at the LQ latent, so that the flow starts from the LQ image rather than from pure noise and preserves the structural priors already present in the input. To achieve high-quality single-step generation, we further propose a simple yet effective training strategy that combines the supervision loss on the single-step output with the conditional flow matching (CFM) loss, enabling high-quality restoration with an arbitrary number of steps without resorting to distillation or other acceleration techniques. Extensive experiments show that RFMSR achieves competitive results against state-of-the-art methods. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EdgeZSAD: Value-Range Quantization for Deploying Zero-Shot Anomaly Detection on Edge Neural Processing Units](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16119)**  `arXiv:2606.16119`  
  _Taewan Cho, Seungmin Lee, Jiho Seo, Andrew Jaeyong Choi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantizing zero-shot industrial anomaly detectors for edge neural processing units can degrade defect detection and localization. We present EdgeZSAD (Edge Zero-Shot Anomaly Detection), a deployment framework whose methodological contribution is Value-Range Quantization (VRQ). We diagnose how attention Value tensors inherit overly wide ranges from fused query-key-value projections and assign independent ranges calibrated on source data, without retraining or changing quantized weights. The engineering application is native deployment of a foundation-model detector for industrial inspection, evaluated on three public benchmarks and Qualcomm edge devices. Under minimum-maximum and mean-squared-error calibration, VRQ improves image-level discrimination and per-region overlap. In the primary calibration set, it recovers 90-98% of the loss in combined image and pixel discrimination relative to half precision, with a small increase in latency over baseline quantization. Matched tensor overrides reproduce the recovery without adding graph nodes, identifying independent Value-range assignment as the effective intervention in the evaluated runtime family.
  </details>

- **[InsHuman: Towards Natural and Identity-Preserving Human Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07402)**  `arXiv:2605.07402`  
  _Jie Li, Shulian Zhang, Wenbo Li, Jian Chen, Yong Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human insertion aims to naturally place specific individuals into a target background. Although existing image editing models may have such ability, they often produce failure cases, including inappropriate human pose in new background, inconsistent number of people, and modified facial identity. Moreover, publicly available human datasets often lack full-body portraits and realistic physical interaction between humans and their background. To address these challenges, we propose InsHuman for natural and identity-preserving human insertion. Specifically, we propose Human-Background Adaptive Fusion (HBAF), which detects foreground humans to obtain a binary mask and applies region-aware weighting to align the human regions between predicted and ground-truth latents, ensuring the person's pose, count, and overall appearance are coherently adapted to the targetthis http URLfurther propose Face-to-Face ID-Preserving (FFIP), which detects and matches faces between the generated image and the source image in terms of face recognition features to enforce identity consistency for eachthis http URLaddition, we propose Bidirectional Data Pairing (BDP) strategy to construct BDP-InsHuman, a high-quality dataset with realistic human-background interactions. Experiments demonstrate that InsHuman achieves significant improvements in generating plausible images while keeping human identity unchanged.
  </details>

- **[Exposing and Mitigating Temporal Attack in Deepfake Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07398)**  `arXiv:2605.07398`  
  _Zheyuan Gu, Minghao Shao, Zhen Wang, Keyu Mao, Ailiang Lin, Mingyou Liang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While spatiotemporal deepfake detectors achieve high AUC, our experiments reveal their susceptibility to evasion attacks. These models tend to overfit on fragile temporal spectrum cues, rather than learning robust semantic causality. To mitigate this vulnerability, we propose SpInShield, a temporal spectral-invariant defense framework explicitly designed to decouple semantic motion from manipulatable spectral artifacts. We propose a learnable spectral adversary that dynamically synthesizes severe spectral deformations, simulating extreme attack scenarios. By employing a shortcut suppression optimization strategy, SpInShield compels the encoder to extract reliable forensic cues while purging unstable spectral statistics from the latent space. Experiments show that SpInShield obtains competitive performance on widely used datasets and outperforms the strongest baseline by 21.30 percentage points in AUC under simulated amplitude spectral attacks.
  </details>

- **[Open-Set Supervised 3D Anomaly Detection: An Industrial Dataset and a Generalisable Framework for Unknown Defects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01171)**  `arXiv:2604.01171`  
  _Hanzhe Liang, Luocheng Zhang, Junyang Xia, HanLiang Zhou, Bingyang Guo, Yingxi Xie, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although self-supervised 3D anomaly detection assumes that acquiring high-precision point clouds is computationally expensive, in real manufacturing scenarios it is often feasible to collect a limited number of anomalous samples. Therefore, we study open-set supervised 3D anomaly detection, where the model is trained with only normal samples and a small number of known anomalous samples, aiming to identify unknown anomalies at test time. We present Open-Industry, a high-quality industrial dataset containing 20 categories, each with five real anomaly types collected from production lines. We first adapt general open-set anomaly detection methods to accommodate 3D point cloud inputs better. Building upon this, we propose Open3D-AD, a point-cloud-oriented approach that leverages normal samples, simulated anomalies, and partially observed real anomalies to model the probability density distributions of normal and anomalous data. Then, we introduce a simple Correspondence Distributions Subsampling to reduce the overlap between normal and non-normal distributions, enabling stronger dual distributions modeling. Based on these contributions, we establish a comprehensive benchmark and evaluate the proposed method extensively on Open-Industry as well as established datasets including Real3D-AD and Anomaly-ShapeNet. Benchmark results and ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of Open3D-AD and further reveal the potential of open-set supervised 3D anomaly detection.
  </details>

- **[Comparative Analysis of 3D Convolutional and 2.5D Slice-Conditioned U-Net Architectures for MRI Super-Resolution via Elucidated Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14667)**  `arXiv:2603.14667`  
  _Hendrik Chiche, Eshaan Gulati, Ludovic Corcos, Logan Rouge_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) super-resolution (SR) methods that computationally enhance low-resolution acquisitions offer a software alternative to higher-field scanners. We study an elucidated diffusion model (EDM) framework for 2x in-plane brain MRI SR and compare two U-Net backbones: (i) a 3D convolutional U-Net that processes volumetric patches and (ii) a 2.5D slice-conditioned U-Net that super-resolves each slice while conditioning on its two neighbouring slices. All models are trained from scratch to convergence on 500 subjects of the NKI cohort of FOMO60K, with a separate 6-subject validation set for checkpoint selection, and evaluated on 70 held-out subjects with subject-level statistics against baselines (EDSR, Swin2SR, Res-SRDiff) trained on the same data. Neither architecture is better overall. On the degradation used in training the two are tied (37.72 dB PSNR for the 3D model and 37.70 dB for the 2.5D model), 0.2 dB ahead of EDSR. When the test degradation changes to k-space truncation, EDSR matches the diffusion models, and with added noise the 2.5D model is the most accurate method. When the test cohort changes to 70 subjects of an external dataset (IXI), the 3D model is the most accurate, 0.4 dB ahead of the best baseline and 0.9 dB ahead of the 2.5D model in foreground PSNR.
  </details>

- **[Free-GVC: Toward Retraining-Free Extreme Generative Video Compression with Temporal Coherence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09868)**  `arXiv:2602.09868`  
  _Xiaoyue Ling, Chuqin Zhou, Chunyi Li, Yunuo Chen, Yuan Tian, Guo Lu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building on recent advances in video generation, generative video compression has emerged as a new paradigm for achieving visually pleasing reconstructions. However, existing methods exhibit limited exploitation of temporal correlations, causing noticeable flicker and degraded temporal coherence at ultra-low bitrates. In this paper, we propose Free-GVC, a retraining-free generative video compression framework that reformulates video coding as latent trajectory compression guided by a video diffusion prior. Our method operates at the group-of-pictures (GOP) level, encoding video segments into a compact latent space and progressively compressing them along the diffusion trajectory. To ensure perceptually consistent reconstruction across GOPs, we introduce an Adaptive Quality Control module that dynamically constructs an online rate-perception surrogate model to predict the optimal diffusion step for each GOP. In addition, an Inter-GOP Alignment module establishes frame overlap and performs latent fusion between adjacent groups, thereby mitigating flicker and enhancing temporal coherence. Experiments show that Free-GVC achieves an average of 93.29% BD-Rate reduction in DISTS over the recent neural codec DCVC-RT, and a user study further confirms its superior perceptual quality and temporal coherence at ultra-low bitrates.
  </details>

- **[Tracing 3D Anatomy in 2D Strokes: Can Projection-Derived Masks Replace 3D Segmentation for Cervical Spine Fracture Recognition?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15235)**  `arXiv:2601.15235`  
  _Fabi Nahian Madhurja, Rusab Sarmun, Muhammad E. H. Chowdhury, Adam Mushtak, Israa Al-Hashimi, Sohaib Bassam Zoghoul_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cervical spine fractures require rapid and accurate diagnosis, yet automatic CT interpretation remains challenging as subtle injuries must be assessed across large 3D volumes. We ask whether full 3D vertebra segmentation is necessary for fracture recognition, or whether vertebra masks approximated from 2D projections preserve sufficient diagnostic context. In the proposed multi-stage pipeline, a YOLOv8 detector localizes the cervical spine from multi-view variance projections (3D mean Intersection over Union 94.45%). Multi-label vertebra segmentation is performed with a DenseNet121-Unet on energy-based sagittal and coronal projections (mean Dice 87.86%). The predicted 2D masks are back-projected and fused into approximate 3D masks per vertebra to extract volumes of interest from the original CT, which are analyzed by an ensemble of 2.5D spatio-sequential CNN-Transformer models. On the full RSNA 2022 cohort at native prevalence (2,019 patients, five-fold cross-validation), the pipeline yields vertebra-level and patient-level F1-scores of 68.15% and 82.28%, area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 91.63% and 90.95%, and area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC) of 75.64% and 92.00%. With the classifier architecture fixed, replacing 3D-segmentation masks by projection-derived masks changed vertebra-level and patient-level AUPRC by +1.03 and +0.65 points, and the pipeline differed from the reproduced RSNA 2022 winning solution by -0.55 and +0.15 points. No AUPRC difference was statistically significant, and fold-wise 95% confidence intervals excluded vertebra-level losses larger than 1.46 and 2.81 points, respectively. Explainability and interobserver analyses examine reliability. Projection-based mask approximation is therefore a viable proxy for 3D vertebra segmentation in cervical fracture recognition.
  </details>

- **[ISLA: Ensemble learning and domain adaptation for generalizable acute ischemic stroke lesion segmentation in diffusion MRI with clinical validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08732)**  `arXiv:2601.08732`  
  _Vincent Roca, Hakim Daouairi, Sevak Yepremian, Hilde Henon, Nicolas Martinage, Laurent Puy, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate segmentation of acute ischemic stroke (AIS) lesions on MRI is essential for clinical decision-making. Deep learning methods offer a promising approach to automating this task. However, they often generalize poorly to unseen clinical sites, and few AIS lesion segmentation methods have been externally validated. In this study, we propose $\mathit{ISLA}$ (Ischemic Stroke Lesion Analyzer), a framework for AIS lesion segmentation built on the design principles of $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$. $\mathit{ISLA}$ combines attention modules, deep supervision, and ensemble learning across different input resolutions, convolutional block types, and loss functions. We also introduce $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$, a variant that leverages unlabeled target-domain images through semi-supervised learning. We trained both approaches on the SOOP and ISLES 2022 datasets and evaluated on a held-out internal validation set of 253 cases, as well as on 590 cases from an independent multicenter clinical cohort. We compared them with three state-of-the-art pre-trained models and with $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$ trained on the same data. $\mathit{ISLA}$ outperformed the pre-trained models on the internal validation set, while both $\mathit{ISLA}$ and $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$ outperformed them on the external cohort. Although $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$ performed slightly better than $\mathit{ISLA}$ internally, both $\mathit{ISLA}$ variants demonstrated better generalization to the external cohort. Furthermore, $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$ significantly improved performance over $\mathit{ISLA}$ on the external cohort. The proposed models performed particularly well on low-contrast lesions, and qualitative error analyses suggested greater robustness to acquisition artifacts. Both models are fast ($\sim$ 5 s per case), and their code and weights are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Vision-language models lag human performance on physical dynamics and intent reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01547)**  `arXiv:2601.01547`  
  _Tianjun Gu, Jingyu Gong, Zhizhong Zhang, Yuan Xie, Lizhuang Ma, Xin Tan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial intelligence is central to embodied cognition, yet contemporary AI systems still struggle to reason about physical interactions in open-world human environments. Despite strong performance on controlled benchmarks, vision-language models often fail to jointly model physical dynamics, reference frames, and the latent human intentions that drive spatial change. We introduce Teleo-Spatial Intelligence (TSI), which captures an important aspect of spatial reasoning: integrating physical dynamics and action goals to reason about actions and their spatial consequences. To evaluate TSI, we present EscherVerse, a large-scale open-world resource built from 11,328 real-world videos, including an 8,000-example benchmark and a 35,963-example instruction-tuning set. Across 27 state-of-the-art vision-language models and an independent analysis of first-pass human responses from 11 annotators, we identify a persistent teleo-spatial reasoning gap: the strongest proprietary model achieves 57.26% overall accuracy, far below first-pass human performance, which ranges from 84.81% to 95.14% with a mean of 90.62%. Fine-tuning on real-world, intent-aware data narrows this gap for open-weight models, but does not close it. EscherVerse provides a diagnostic testbed for purpose-aware spatial reasoning and highlights a critical gap between pattern recognition and human-level understanding in embodied AI.
  </details>

- **[LatentFM: A Latent Flow Matching Approach for Generative Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04821)**  `arXiv:2512.04821`  
  _Ngoc Huynh Trinh, Hoang Anh Nguyen Kim, Hai Toan Nguyen, Quoc Long Tran_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative models have achieved remarkable progress with the emergence of flow matching (FM). It has demonstrated strong generative capabilities and attracted significant attention as a simulation-free flow-based framework capable of learning exact data densities. Motivated by these advances, we propose LatentFM, a flow-based model operating in the latent space for medical image segmentation. To model the data distribution, we first design two variational autoencoders (VAEs) to encode both medical images and their corresponding masks into a lower-dimensional latent space. We then estimate a conditional velocity field that guides the flow based on the input image. By sampling multiple latent representations, our method synthesizes diverse segmentation outputs whose pixel-wise variance reliably captures the underlying data distribution, enabling both highly accurate and uncertainty-aware predictions. Furthermore, we generate confidence maps that quantify the model certainty, providing clinicians with richer information for deeper analysis. We conduct experiments on two datasets, ISIC-2018 and CVC-Clinic, and compare our method with several prior baselines, including both deterministic and generative approach models. Through comprehensive evaluations, both qualitative and quantitative results show that our approach achieves superior segmentation accuracy while remaining highly efficient in the latent space.
  </details>

- **[Click, Predict, Trust: Clinician-Guided and Clinician-Evaluated AI Segmentation for Lung Cancer CT-Based Prognosis within the Knowledge-to-Action Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17039)**  `arXiv:2510.17039`  
  _Mohammad R. Salmanpour, Sonya Falahati, Amir Hossein Pouria, Amin Mousavi, Somayeh Sadat Mehrnia, Morteza Alizadeh, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lung cancer remains the leading cause of cancer mortality, with CT imaging central to screening, prognosis, and treatment. Manual segmentation is variable and time-intensive, while deep learning (DL) offers automation but faces barriers to clinical adoption. Guided by the Knowledge-to-Action framework, this study develops a clinician-in-the-loop DL pipeline to enhance reproducibility, prognostic accuracy, and clinical trust. Multi-center CT data from 999 patients across 12 public datasets were analyzed using five DL models (3D Attention U-Net, ResUNet, VNet, ReconNet, SAM-Med3D), benchmarked against expert contours on whole and click-point cropped images. Segmentation reproducibility was assessed using 497 PySERA-extracted radiomic features via Spearman correlation, ICC, Wilcoxon tests, and MANOVA, while prognostic modeling compared supervised (SL) and semi-supervised learning (SSL) across 38 dimensionality reduction strategies and 24 classifiers. Six physicians qualitatively evaluated masks across seven domains, including clinical meaningfulness, boundary quality, prognostic value, trust, and workflow integration. VNet achieved the best performance (Dice = 0.83, IoU = 0.71), radiomic stability (mean correlation = 0.76, ICC = 0.65), and predictive accuracy under SSL (accuracy = 0.88, F1 = 0.83). SSL consistently outperformed SL across models. Radiologists favored VNet for peritumoral representation and smoother boundaries, preferring AI-generated initial masks for refinement rather than replacement. These results demonstrate that integrating VNet with SSL yields accurate, reproducible, and clinically trusted CT-based lung cancer prognosis, highlighting a feasible path toward physician-centered AI translation.
  </details>

- **[BusterX++: Towards Unified Cross-Modal AI-Generated Content Detection and Explanation with MLLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14632)**  `arXiv:2507.14632`  
  _Haiquan Wen, Tianxiao Li, Zhenglin Huang, Yiwei He, Guangliang Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid advancement of generative AI has substantially improved image and video synthesis, amplifying the risk of multimodal visual misinformation. Recent MLLMs have shown promise for transparent AI-generated content detection through reasoning and explanation, yet existing approaches largely treat image and video forensics as isolated tasks, leaving cross-modal synergies underexplored. To address this, we present \textbf{BusterX++}, a unified MLLM for joint image and video detection with interpretable reasoning. We also introduce \textbf{GenBuster-Bench++}, a curated benchmark containing balanced image and video samples spanning recent generation models and diverse real-world scenarios, with synthetic images and videos screened using the same human difficulty criterion. Using this controlled setting, we revisit the widely adopted SFT+RL post-training paradigm. Notably, in the unified image-video setting, our results demonstrate that a single-stage pure RL strategy driven primarily by sparse outcome rewards outperforms a strong SFT+RL baseline. We observe that single-stage pure RL maintains higher policy entropy than SFT-initialized RL throughout training. This observation suggests a possible role for exploration in the performance gains observed under unified image-video training. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BusterX++ achieves state-of-the-art performance, highlighting the powerful potential of RL for unified cross-modal visual reasoning.
  </details>

- **[When No-Reference Image Quality Models Meet MAP Estimation in Diffusion Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.06406)**  `arXiv:2403.06406`  
  _Weixia Zhang, Dingquan Li, Guangtao Zhai, Xiaokang Yang, Kede Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contemporary no-reference image quality assessment (NR-IQA) models can effectively quantify perceived image quality, often achieving strong correlations with human perceptual scores on standard IQA benchmarks. Yet, limited efforts have been devoted to treating NR-IQA models as natural image priors for real-world image enhancement, and consequently comparing them from a perceptual optimization standpoint. In this work, we show -- for the first time -- that NR-IQA models can be plugged into the maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimation framework for image enhancement. This is achieved by performing gradient ascent in the diffusion latent space rather than in the raw pixel domain, leveraging a pretrained differentiable and bijective diffusion process. Likely, different NR-IQA models lead to different enhanced outputs, which in turn provides a new computational means of comparing them. Unlike conventional correlation-based measures, our comparison method offers complementary insights into the respective strengths and weaknesses of the competing NR-IQA models in perceptual optimization scenarios. Additionally, we aim to improve the best-performing NR-IQA model in diffusion latent MAP estimation by incorporating the advantages of other top-performing methods. The resulting model delivers noticeably better results in enhancing real-world images afflicted by unknown and complex distortions, all preserving a high degree of image fidelity.
  </details>

- **[WeightBench: Evaluating Language Models as Weight-Space Programmers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18553)**  `arXiv:2611.18553`  
  _Aayush Marasini, Barsat Khadka, Nick Rahimi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can operate directly on the parameters of other neural networks. They can read what a network was trained to do, fix a network that fails, and write working networks from scratch, all without gradient descent. This capability has been termed Weight-Space Programming (WSP). Exploring this new paradigm systematically requires a robust benchmark. We introduce WEIGHTBENCH, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs of increasing capability on 42 WSP tasks across five tracks: reading, fixing and writing weights, recognizing edits that are impossible, and data-free compression. The networks whose weights the LLMs read and edit range from 2K-parameter control policies to 7B-parameter language models. Experimental benchmark results show that frontier agents can already read small networks and write working control policies without ever running them, but cannot yet patch language models, compress them without data, or tell an impossible edit from a hard one. On all 42 tasks, Claude Opus 5.5 reaches a macro score of 0.535 (Sonnet 5.5 0.466, Haiku 5.5 0.402, DeepSeek v4-flash 0.215); on the 38 tasks it was run on, GPT-6.1-sol reaches 0.568 against Opus's 0.546. Code, the task registry, our OS-level sandbox harness, and the live leaderboard are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sample-efficient prediction of high-entropy alloy strength through outcome-aware robust sequential learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18462)**  `arXiv:2611.18462`  
  _Ahmed Shoyeb Raihan, Md Habibor Rahman, Ashish Nimbarte, Imtiaz Ahmed_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate prediction of high-entropy alloy (HEA) strength can guide composition and processing decisions; however, obtaining tensile-test data for HEAs can be costly and experimentally demanding. This study proposes Robust Outcome-aware Sequential Experimentation for High-Entropy Alloys (ROSE-HEA), a sample-efficient framework for predicting ultimate tensile strength (UTS) from alloy composition and processing conditions. Its acquisition policy anticipates how possible UTS outcomes update predictions and the support for complementary Gaussian process (GP) descriptions. It then reassesses the worst-case expected predictive risk remaining across a reference domain to select informative candidates. A retrospective evaluation on 501 HEA records compares ROSE-HEA with expected-improvement (EI) and upper-confidence-bound (UCB) Bayesian optimization (BO), random GP sampling, and eight conventional regressors across 30 matched runs with shared initial observations and test conditions. Starting from 25 initial observations and acquiring 100 additional responses, ROSE-HEA achieves the lowest mean root-mean-square error (RMSE), at 152.84 MPa, and the highest mean R^2, at 0.7443. Mean RMSE is reduced by 6.16% and 6.37% relative to EI and UCB, respectively. The selected observations improve prediction across six regressor families and provide broader design-space coverage. ROSE-HEA also surpasses the strongest conventional regressor's mean predictive performance using 125 rather than 185 observations, corresponding to 32.4% fewer training observations. These findings demonstrate the potential of outcome-aware robust sequential learning to improve UTS prediction in HEAs under constrained training budgets.
  </details>

- **[Black-Box Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Models via Ensemble-of-Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18453)**  `arXiv:2611.18453`  
  _Wang Ma, Debarun Bhattacharjya, Junkyu Lee, Nhan H Pham, Harsha Kokel, Qiang Ji_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable use of large language models (LLMs) requires uncertainty estimates that separate ambiguity intrinsic to a prompt from genuine knowledge gaps. Bayesian and deep-ensemble methods deliver this aleatoric/epistemic decomposition in principle, but are computationally prohibitive at LLM scale and require white-box access. Existing black-box methods, in contrast, summarize to a single scalar that cannot make the distinction. We introduce a two-level ensemble that bridges this gap. Stochastic decoding of a fixed prompt forms the inner ensemble; meaning-preserving semantic perturbations of the prompt form the outer ensemble. Variability is measured in a continuous embedding space, and the law of total covariance gives an exact within-/between-perturbation decomposition to estimate aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty (AU/EU); it requires only black-box sampling access. On top of this estimator, we contribute three theoretical results that ground the framework: (i) a finite-sample bias identity for nested Monte Carlo with a closed-form bias-corrected epistemic estimator; (ii) a Bayesian bridge bounding the gap between perturbation-population AU/EU and Bayesian targets; and (iii) a separation result identifying a class of failure modes that any consistency-only black-box method provably misses while perturbation-based EU detects. Across five short QA benchmarks and four instruction-tuned LLMs spanning two families and two scales, our estimator is competitive with strong white-box baselines, and at a matched generation budget it matches leading black-box methods on error ranking while decomposing AU and EU; controlled paired interventions show that AU and EU respond selectively to distinct, actionable sources of failure: prompt ambiguity and model knowledge gaps; and a long-form pilot on the NQ long-answer split shows the framework extends to claim-level factuality without retraining.
  </details>

- **[Deep Neural Networks Training Efficiency: Does Grokking Modify its Dynamics?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18396)**  `arXiv:2611.18396`  
  _Eduardo Cueto-Mendoza, John D. Kelleher_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study whether grokking, the delayed emergence of out-of-distribution generalization after apparent overfitting, alters training efficiency in deep neural networks, where efficiency is measured as accuracy per unit energy. Using two canonical settings, modular arithmetic and MNIST, we analyze two neural network models under 16-, 32-, and 64-bit precision and use Orthograd to test whether grokking produces noticeable deviations from the typically decreasing dynamics of training efficiency. We consider both short training runs of up to 500 epochs and long training runs of up to 20,000 epochs, and in both cases identify grokking as a sharp improvement in test accuracy after a sustained train-test gap. In the short-horizon experiments, applying Orthograd at progressively stronger levels during early training does not induce grokking, and efficiency remains smoothly decreasing. In the long-horizon experiments, Orthograd induces grokking, but the efficiency curves remain approximately monotonic and show no appreciable local increase at the grokking transition. These results suggest that, in our setting, grokking does not materially alter overall training-efficiency dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Where Tree Ensembles Decide: Structural Local Explanation with Decision Predicate Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18382)**  `arXiv:2611.18382`  
  _Sylvio Barbon Junior, Luca Longo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tree ensembles are among the most accurate models for tabular data, yet the computation behind a single prediction is spread over hundreds of decision rules. Feature attributions and counterfactuals explain such predictions through the influence of features or a change of the input, leaving open which of the thresholds applied by the trees a prediction responds to, while structural explanations compress these rules and lose the trees behind them. To address this gap, we propose a local Decision Predicate Graph (local DPG), which gathers the rules executed for a sample into a verifiable record of the computation, with a class support that reproduces every explained prediction. From these rules we select the critical predicate, the executed predicate whose crossing is estimated to move the model most strongly toward a chosen class, which offers the user a concrete threshold and the trees that share it, without an external explainer. Across 16 datasets and four ensemble families, it separates differently predicted neighbours better than random and nearest-threshold predicates. A single crossing recovers the true class in 46-69% of wrong predictions, against 29-50% when the same rule is applied to the feature ranked first by TreeSHAP, a measure close to the selection criterion, and on planted faults it finds the faulty threshold in 46-71% of the resulting errors, against 6-21% at random. Although training-free edits of these predicates did not reduce the error on unseen data, the critical predicate complements attributions and counterfactuals by naming where a prediction can be changed.
  </details>

- **[Emulating physics-based XCO2 retrievals across OCO-2 viewing modes with compact neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18333)**  `arXiv:2611.18333`  
  _Xianlei San, Chengxing Zhai, Hui Su, Kuai Dai, Shuangchen Du_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Operational full-physics retrieval of the column-averaged dry-air mole fraction of carbon dioxide from satellite spectra is computationally demanding. We use singular value decomposition to compress spectra from the Orbiting Carbon Observatory-2 separately by band. Compact multilayer perceptrons use the resulting spectral scores, prior information, and viewing geometry to predict the difference between the full-physics retrieval and its operational prior. Adding the predicted difference to the prior yields an estimate of the retrieved mole fraction. The models were trained on 2015-2019 observations and validated on 2020 observations. In 2021-2025 tests, the mean annual root-mean-square errors relative to the Atmospheric Carbon Dioxide Observations from Space retrieval product were 0.89, 1.35, and 1.43 parts per million for ocean glint, land glint, and nadir observations, respectively. On East Asian nadir observations screened using the published criteria for the spectral Transformer, our model yielded a lower mean annual error than reported for that model in 2020-2022. These results show that compact neural networks using compressed spectra can efficiently emulate the full-physics retrieval across multiple viewing modes and years.
  </details>

- **[Right Token, Right Time: From Per-Token Gating to Trajectory-Aware Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18274)**  `arXiv:2611.18274`  
  _Nima Eshraghi, Lovedeep Gondara, Sagarika Suresh, Xiaotong Xu, Jithin Pradeep, Fanny Chevalier_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering via sparse autoencoders (SAEs) enables behavioral control of large language models without task-specific fine-tuning. Yet, standard methods apply the steering signal at every generated token, displacing representations from the distribution the model was trained on and eroding generation quality. We show that \emph{when} along a generation the intervention is applied is a decisive axis of control. We cast steering as a per-token allocation problem under a fixed budget of steerable tokens, so that methods differ only in which tokens they select, making allocation the sole variable. Within this framework we introduce two gates. The first, Contrastive Feature Projection (CFP), is memoryless and selects the tokens least aligned with the target behavior through a sparse, interpretable feature projection. The second, Closed-Loop Adaptive Bernoulli Steering (CLABS), is trajectory aware. It treats allocation as closed-loop feedback control, tracking the alignment a generation has accumulated so far and spending budget where the trajectory falls behind. On a linear steering-response model we derive an explicit gain region that guarantees closed-loop stability of CLABS and show that its integral term reduces the steady-state tracking offset. Empirically, CFP and CLABS achieve the largest behavioral shift among budgeted gates at matched budgets, with CLABS strongest under the tight budgets where allocation matters most, while best preserving fluency.
  </details>

- **[NeoResearch: Literature- and Experience-Guided Autonomous Time Series Model Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18235)**  `arXiv:2611.18235`  
  _Ze Guo, Mingyue Cheng, Xiaoyu Tao, Shuo Yu, Shangtong Ye_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing effective forecasting models for diverse time-series domains requires substantial expert knowledge, evidence from scientific literature on the web, and iterative experimentation. However, translating this knowledge and research experience into model designs requires preserving source context and testing applicability under task-specific assumptions. In this paper, we propose NeoResearch, a literature- and experience-guided autonomous research framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) as researchers to discover lightweight, domain-specific forecasting models. Specifically, NeoResearch establishes an expert-inspired dual-loop research workflow, coupling hypothesis-driven architecture exploration with iterative training and hyperparameter optimization through experimental feedback. To enable knowledge-grounded discovery, the agent autonomously mines scientific literature from the Web, extracting modeling mechanisms, empirical evidence, and applicability conditions to formulate task-grounded, testable architecture hypotheses. Furthermore, a hierarchical experience memory links literature findings to experimental outcomes and consolidates conditional lessons, guiding architecture discovery and training optimization while supporting reuse across forecasting tasks. Experiments on ten datasets show competitive performance and support dual loops, literature extraction, and memory reuse. Our code is available.
  </details>

- **[T2ST: Data-Efficient Spatio-Temporal Foundation Models via Temporal Data and Knowledge Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18199)**  `arXiv:2611.18199`  
  _Yue Xu, Kaiyun Wei, Wenying Duan, Zichen Xu, Shuai Ma, Xiaoxi He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatio-temporal foundation models (STFMs) require large-scale training corpora, yet synchronized observations and spatial metadata are costly to acquire and curate, limiting the availability of suitable data. By contrast, time-series data are abundant and easier to collect. Since observations at each location form a time series, this motivates a natural question: can abundant time-series data enhance STFMs when spatio-temporal observations are scarce? To bridge this gap, we propose T2ST, a data-efficient training framework that augments limited spatio-temporal observations with selected auxiliary time series and transfers knowledge from a frozen time-series foundation model. Our key insight is that temporal pattern learning can benefit from abundant external time series, while spatial dependency learning remains grounded in real spatio-temporal observations. Guided by this insight, T2ST retrieves matched sequences to reinforce sparsely observed temporal patterns and selects reliable complementary sequences to broaden temporal coverage. Temporal relation distillation aligns relationships among historical patches, while multi-scale prediction distillation transfers forecasting behavior at fine and coarse temporal resolutions, enabling knowledge transfer across heterogeneous architectures. Using only 10\% of the original spatio-temporal data, T2ST enables STFMs to achieve overall forecasting performance competitive with or superior to their full-data counterparts across six long-term and twelve short-term forecasting datasets. These results establish a new paradigm for data-efficient training of spatio-temporal foundation models. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MarathonTS: a benchmark of long synthetic time series from window assembly and long-series generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18190)**  `arXiv:2611.18190`  
  _Ilyas Varshavskiy, Sergey Shulgin, Shuhrat Khalilbekov, Bonu Boboeva, Azizjon Azimi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many applications of synthetic time series require a series that is much longer than the windows on which a generative model is trained. Such a series can be assembled from a pool of short generated windows, which requires deciding which windows are taken, in what order, and how their seams are processed, or it can be produced directly by a long-series generator. This study presents MarathonTS, to our knowledge the first open benchmark that compares both families under one protocol. Every method produces a series of the same length on 9 open hourly datasets with 10 seeds. The series is evaluated by its fidelity to the real data (83 metrics in 12 groups, against three real references: train, validation and an out-of-time split), by its utility (models are trained on the synthetic series only and tested on real data, TSTR against TRTR, in 14 classification and regression tasks), by its memorisation of the training data and by its computational cost. The statistical analysis is carried out as a separate stage that reads the published tables and gives the rankings of every method in every slice, the answers to the research questions with tests and uncertainty, and the results of a pre-specified confirmatory family. In the main run, the methods differ in both confirmatory cells; the choice of windows matters more than the processing of their seams, which does not improve the series, and no method trains the downstream models as well as the real series. The top tier of the leaderboard is reproduced on other hardware, whereas the exact places within it are not. For the reproducibility of the study, the code, the data hashes and all results are publicly available (links below).
  </details>

- **[On the Limits of Sufficient Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18163)**  `arXiv:2611.18163`  
  _Klaus Weinbauer, Peter F. Stadler, Thomas Gärtner, Sagar Malhotra_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We formally characterize the conditions under which sufficient explanations exist for classifiers on structured data such as graphs and strings. We focus on post-hoc explainers that select parts of the input as sufficient explanations. An explanation is sufficient if the selected part alone ensures the predicted label. Sufficient explanations have been widely adopted across data modalities, including graphs, strings, and images. However, a unified formal analysis of sufficient explanations remains missing. We bridge this gap with four main contributions: (i) We define a general explanation formalism consisting of an instance space, a classifier, and a select relation. The select relation specifies which potential sub-instances or feature subsets can be selected as explanations from each instance; (ii) We show that the existence of sufficient explainers is fully characterized by the interplay between the classifier and the select relation; (iii) We extend this analysis to practically relevant variants of sufficiency, including probabilistic sufficiency and deletion sufficiency; (iv) Based on the formal analysis, we devise statistics that bound the maximum achievable sufficiency for a given task. We evaluate these statistics on language, protein, and molecular classification datasets and find that full sufficiency is unattainable on most of them. Since these bounds hold for every explainer, they provide a task-level reference against which any explainer's sufficiency can be judged in absolute terms.
  </details>

- **[Which Model, For Which Variable? Per-Variable Forecast Combination in Sparse ICU Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18149)**  `arXiv:2611.18149`  
  _Marius Fracarolli, Stefan Riezler_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical forecasting papers typically report a single best architecture. We show that on multivariate ICU physiology this question conceals variable-specific differences: no architecture is best on every variable, and which model wins changes from variable to variable. Across five ICU databases (MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, eICU, HiRID, and UMM (University Medicine Mannheim), a German single-center ICU) we forecast 94 channels over a 24-hour horizon with a pool of 14 models, and combine them with weights fitted per variable. On test, the combination beats the best single model on all five databases by 6.1-11.0% mean squared error (mean 8.7%). The gain is concentrated where clinical data is thinnest: the sparsest third of channels improves by 11.9% and laboratory values by 7.5%, against 3.1% for the densest third. Rank-based harmonic weighting of the best five models per variable, with no fitted weights at all, recovers about four-fifths of the gain. Extending the pool afterwards with a per-channel gradient-boosted tree, ARIMA and ETS makes the tree the validation-selected best single model on all five databases; per-variable combination still improves on it by 2.9-5.9% (significant on four of five), while equal weighting of the same members does not. As on univariate benchmarks, learned combination beats simple averaging - but only once the weights are constrained. We also report which model to prefer when, and show that the ordering changes with database size.
  </details>

- **[STAMP: Manufacturing-Informed Generative Design and Shape Optimization of Sheet Panels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18144)**  `arXiv:2611.18144`  
  _Jihoon Kim, Jun Hyeok Song, Wonho Ha, Namwoo Kang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative design of formed sheet panels must preserve geometric manufacturing conditions while exploring layouts and improving performance. STAMP embeds these conditions directly in the shape representation. Each panel is described by its material layout and a reference surface with one height at each occupied position in a plane perpendicular to the drawing direction. Nominal thickness is prescribed along the normal. The representation allows curved surfaces, ribs, and openings. It excludes reference-surface undercuts and keeps nominal thickness fixed without manufacturing penalty terms. Because these conditions belong to the representation, every generated and optimized panel satisfies them regardless of training-set size. Boundary-conditioned diffusion generates alternative layouts, and predictor gradients guide a surface optimization that retains outlines, openings, and designated fixed regions. We demonstrate STAMP on automotive hood inner panels using CarHoods10k. With a comparable number of stored values, depth maps reconstruct surfaces more accurately than point-cloud and voxel baselines. From only five training designs, one of three training runs yields novel and varied layouts. A generator trained on 500 designs, 6.5% of the training set, already reaches the layout variety of the full-data model. Across 11 held-out design families, predictor-based selection among existing dataset designs yields an average gap of 0.18 mm to the best available FEA deformation, versus an expected 1.59 mm for random selection. It does better than random selection in all 11 families. At a 10% depth-change bound, a network not used in the search predicts an average deformation reduction of 24.8% for six generated and existing panels, with a 3.2% average mass increase computed from geometry. Engineers can thus explore and optimize panels within this manufacturing-informed design space.
  </details>

- **[When Copying Is Hard to Beat: A Negative Result in Clinical Assessment Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18142)**  `arXiv:2611.18142`  
  _Marius Fracarolli, Stefan Riezler_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We report a negative result in next-day clinical assessment forecasting: the evaluated models show no consistent advantage over strong persistence-based baselines. Clinicians can view the previous day's questionnaire answers, a plausible source of documentation persistence. Predicting 45 items on 29,715 ICU day-pairs with patient-disjoint splits, a physiology-informed gated model improves item-macro-F1 over a one-line clinical rule by +0.0019 [+0.0006,+0.0031], while losing accuracy. No macro-F1 advantage is established when the physiology window ends at the previous form's reference time (+0.0001 [-0.0006,+0.0008]), or when scoring excludes the 68.5% of cells derived from parent answers; combining both changes yields a small disadvantage. Four checkpoints across three families underperform persistence, and a fine-tuned one differs from it on only two cells. A categorical read-out raises changed-cell accuracy eightfold, but a state-only read-out scores higher overall than forecast-only decoding and matches a transition matrix. This negative result highlights how the documentation process, input cutoff, observation mask and decoding rule shape conclusions about forecasting recorded assessments.
  </details>

- **[Enrichment-Oriented Virtual Screening of Perovskite Solar Cells through Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18043)**  `arXiv:2611.18043`  
  _Yezeyuan Zheng, Ziyang Hu, Maojun Sun. Ruijian Han, Jian Huang, Guanhua Chen, Jie Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Experimental optimization of perovskite solar cells is constrained by combinatorial diversity in absorber compositions, device layers, and processing, which makes virtual screening attractive for prioritizing recipes before fabrication. We develop a surrogate-guided reinforcement-learning framework that casts recipe generation as virtual screening, enriching candidate shortlists near high-performing experimental neighbors. A frozen Tabular Prior-data Fitted Network (TabPFN) guides recipe generation, while a reference pool of 890 measured devices enables neighborhood-based assessment. Five-fold evaluation on 1,846 devices shows top-ten selection raises mean power conversion efficiency (PCE) from 19.68\% to 23.74\%. Three learned policies are compared with six baselines under 3,000 surrogate evaluations per run. In top-100 shortlists, Proximal policy optimization (PPO) and recurrent PPO (RPPO) achieve neighborhood-supported hit rates of 23\% and 24\% (7.9- and 8.3-fold Pure Random's 2.9\%), versus 10\% (3.4-fold) for the baselines with the highest supported hit rate (L-SHADE and Ax BO, tied) at $K = 100$. Gains coexist with weak-support fractions. Of 9,000 recipes, 65.4\% match a composition family at similarity 0.80, falling to 11.7\% at 0.90. Results support enrichment-oriented prioritization but not experimental performance, seed robustness, or matched-budget advantage.
  </details>

- **[RL$^{\mathrm{RT}}$: On-Device Reinforcement Learning Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18035)**  `arXiv:2611.18035`  
  _Zexin Li, Xinwei Gao, Arvind Easwaran_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-device reinforcement learning runs a periodic actor and a learner on one embedded GPU shared with other workloads. Because the learner produces the actor's weights, existing designs stall the actor or let its policy drift. We present RL$^{\mathrm{RT}}$, an on-device runtime that makes this coupling a tunable parameter: a refresh bound limits actor releases between refreshes, and a GPU permit with slack-aware admission prioritises the actor and fills spare time with training atop any base scheduler. Under the real refresh cost on two Jetson boards, RL$^{\mathrm{RT}}$ keeps a 16.8 M-parameter policy's deadline misses below 1%, against 85 to 100% without it.
  </details>

- **[Conflict, Feasibility, Faithfulness, Displacement: Four Quantities That Come Apart in Federated Dual-Cone Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18014)**  `arXiv:2611.18014`  
  _Grzegorz Gwardys, Karol Wojciechowski, Mateusz Gwardys, Bartosz {Ś}widerski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  FedFV, FedGH and FedMGDA+ aggregate client updates with multi-objective gradient aggregators to remove conflict between them. We examine this rationale with UPGrad, which increases no client's loss to first order, in a comparison that changes only the direction of FedAvg's update. Four quantities the rationale treats as one -- conflict between client updates, feasibility of their weighted mean, faithfulness of the transmitted updates to the clients' gradients, and displacement of the update -- come apart. Under Dirichlet partitioning UPGrad improves CIFAR-10 accuracy by +0.78 pp (14/15 seeds, p < 0.001), only with full participation; on Oxford-IIIT Pet its +0.52 pp gain vanishes (+0.07 pp) once the data our protocol left unused are kept. Varying only the Dirichlet concentration, the gain grows with heterogeneity, yet the split with the most conflict shows none. Uniform client weights, blind to conflict, match the CIFAR-10 gain and exceed the Pets gain; a matched random rotation does neither. No fairness effect survives multiplicity correction, and on CIFAR-10 the guarantee does not reach the clients' gradients. We state what evaluations should report.
  </details>

- **[RiverScan: Learning to Accumulate on River Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18005)**  `arXiv:2611.18005`  
  _He Zhu, Zhiyi Gao, Youhuan Yang, Han Quan, Ming Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate runoff prediction across large river basins requires models to capture heterogeneous local hydrological responses and their accumulation downstream. River-network connectivity provides a natural structure for this task, but using it effectively requires learning how to combine contributions from subcatchments of different sizes. We frame this challenge as learning to accumulate and introduce RiverScan, a spatial operator based on area-weighted selective state-space recurrence. RiverScan recursively combines local temporal representations with accumulated upstream states in topological order. Contributing-area fractions account for subcatchment size, while learned retention controls how upstream information persists downstream. Experiments on 608 stations in the Danube basin show that RiverScan outperforms the evaluated temporal and graph baselines in overall predictive performance. Structural controls and ablations demonstrate that composing heterogeneous subcatchment representations in a manner aligned with hydrological accumulation substantially improves runoff prediction, with the benefits of correct topology growing along the river-network hierarchy. These findings establish physically structured accumulation as an effective inductive bias for hydrological modeling and motivate finer-grained, fully distributed AI-based hydrological models.
  </details>

- **[When Does Oversampling Help? A Decision Framework for Imbalanced Data Streams Under Label Delay and Concept Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17981)**  `arXiv:2611.17981`  
  _Ahmed Awad, Anirudh Sengar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For imbalanced data streams with delayed labels, is it worth adding a streaming oversampler, or does continuous re-evaluation of earlier predictions already recover the cost of the delay? We address this through an extensive experimental comparison of streaming oversampling and continuous re-evaluation under joint class imbalance, concept drift, and label delay. Streaming oversamplers are typically evaluated under the test-then-train assumption of immediate labels; their behaviour under these conditions, assessed through re-evaluation, has not been systematically studied. We close this gap with a decision framework that predicts when oversampling adds value over re-evaluation, organised around three axes: drift density, minority-class learnability, and delay relative to the refinement window, and operationalised as a competition between a recovery channel (re-evaluation) and a minority-coverage channel (oversampling). Evaluating $15$ classifiers on $6$ synthetic and $6$ real streams, we find: re-evaluation recovers delay-induced loss on drift-active streams but is silent on stationary ones; oversampling's marginal contribution over re-evaluation is statistically detectable in only one real-data configuration; and cost--performance Pareto recommendations from synthetic streams do not transfer to real data, where fronts partition by drift regime. Method selection should therefore be conditioned on the stream's profile and re-estimated as it evolves, not inherited from synthetic benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Analyzing Learning-Dynamics Across Loss and Prediction Choices for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17967)**  `arXiv:2611.17967`  
  _Dongwoo Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Denoising generative models predict clean data ($x$), noise ($\epsilon$), or velocity ($v$), while training losses can be measured in any of these spaces. Although the resulting nine combinations are algebraically related, they can yield substantially different denoising accuracy and sample quality. We disentangle the effects of prediction and loss spaces. For a fixed prediction target, we show that changing the loss space is exactly equivalent to timestep reweighting and provide a heuristic analysis of how this reweighting affects generation quality. We then show that a rank-$m$ linear bottleneck compressing a $D$-dimensional noisy input imposes an irreducible squared-error floor of $D-m$ on noise and velocity prediction, whereas clean-data prediction can exploit low-dimensional data structure. Combining this bound with prediction-dependent error conversion yields distinct necessary conditions on bottleneck capacity. Experiments with U-Net and JiT across CIFAR-10, Imagenette, and ImageNet-256 support these theoretical predictions. Under compression, prediction choice strongly affects both denoising accuracy and sample quality, with noise-prediction errors closely matching the theoretical lower bound. Our results provide a quantitative understanding of how architectural bottlenecks interact with prediction and loss choices in denoising generative models.
  </details>

- **[DeltaVisionX: A Low-Cost Vision-Guided Automation System Using YOLOv11 and PLC-Based Actuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17959)**  `arXiv:2611.17959`  
  _Lara Sobh, Marc Nahhas, Francisco Katach El Halabi, Michel Owayjan, Fadi Elias Sleiman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents DeltaVisionX, a low-cost industrial automation system that integrates real-time computer vision with PLC-based actuation for object classification and sorting. The system uses the YOLOv11 (You Only Look Once) deep learning model to detect objects on an operating conveyor belt and communicates detection results to a Delta DVP-SE PLC, which actuates pneumatic pistons for sorting. A custom dataset was created, labeled, and augmented to train the YOLOv11 model, achieving a mean average precision of 0.97 with real-time inference of 100 ms per frame. Encoder-based tracking ensures reliable synchronization between detection and actuation, supporting a throughput of 80 objects per minute. Results show that affordable, locally developed automation can reduce labor dependency and improve manufacturing efficiency. The novelty of the proposed system lies in its encodersynchronized vision-PLC integration rather than in the detection model itself. Future work includes integrating a highspeed Delta robot with EtherCAT-based servo control for advanced pick-and-place operations.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning Models Confabulate to Defend Injected Beliefs About Their Own Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17958)**  `arXiv:2611.17958`  
  _Yang Ou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring and self-report are proposed as levers for AI oversight, on the assumption that a model's account of what it is doing is anchored to what it was asked. We show this anchor can be cut. An activation edit invisible in the prompt text, the injected forming-trace of an off-topic concept, overwrites a reasoning model's belief about its own request: across six models spanning scale, architecture, and recipe, the injected topic becomes the task it reasons about, reports back, and answers: the forced quote-back returns the injected topic in 47 of 48 model-concept cells, and on the four models with clean final haiku the injected answer follows in 99% of generations. Warnings barely move it: even one naming the true topic yields under 5% true-topic answers. Handed the truth in clean tokens, the model confabulates a story about the user ("maybe they got confused and corrected themselves") and answers with the injection anyway, its behavior and chain-of-thought corrupted together: a concrete limit on CoT monitorability. Opening the model up offers no reprieve: it attends to the true request even harder after edits, yet that attention is causally inert, and the injected content rides intact task circuits, so attention maps and circuit attributions read as on-task too; the same edit redirects the request's language and format, not only its topic. What the injection reaches is the model's reference to its own input; what it elicits is its defense of it.
  </details>

- **[Differentially Private Federated Class-Incremental Learning for EEG Mental State Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17944)**  `arXiv:2611.17944`  
  _Baran Can Gül, Ali Gülümser, Nasser Jazdi, Michael Weyrich_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated learning (FL) under differential privacy (DP) is typically evaluated on large client cohorts, where subsampling and cross-client averaging amplify privacy and smooth injected noise. Personal biosignal applications such as wearable EEG instead involve small federations of a handful of subjects, a regime whose interaction with formal client-level DP is largely uncharacterized. We study differentially private federated class-incremental learning in this small-cohort regime, combining a public EEG dataset with a custom four-channel MUSE 2 recording set into a K=5-client federation, and give a masked per-class training protocol that avoids degenerate gradients and missing negative supervision under class-incremental streaming. We propose FedEEG, combining Synaptic-Intelligence consolidation with a lagged adaptive FedProx term, and show it reduces catastrophic forgetting relative to FedAvg, FedProx, and a federated Learning-without-Forgetting baseline; on-device balanced-batch replay reduces mean forgetting by a further 56.7%. A class-scoping diagnosis yields $50.5\%\pm3.6\%$ accuracy with 6 seeds and 2.02 times chance. We also introduce head-scoped client-level DP, which protects the classification head and alone reduces the achievable $\varepsilon$ by 200 times at a matched round budget; combined with fewer communication rounds it reaches $\varepsilon\!\approx\!10.4$ with utility not statistically distinguishable from non-private training.
  </details>

- **[Quantum Graph Neural Networks: Architectures, Trainability, and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17886)**  `arXiv:2611.17886`  
  _Zeinab Sharifi, Hossein Shirazi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantum graph neural networks (QGNNs) apply parametrized quantum circuits to graph-structured data, with the aim of overcoming limits of classical graph neural networks in scalability, expressivity, and long-range information flow. Since the first Hamiltonian-based model in 2019, the field has produced many architectures, applications, and claims, but the evidence behind them has not been assessed systematically. This survey introduces a unified formalism that describes every QGNN as an encoder, a graph-conditioned circuit, observables, and an optional classical head, together with a circuit-level condition for permutation equivariance and a resource model for comparing costs. We organize the literature into five architecture families (convolutional, equivariant, message-passing and recurrent, Hamiltonian, and hybrid) and compare them by qubit cost, symmetry, scalability, and evidence. We then review symmetry and geometric inductive biases (permutation, rotation, and Lorentz), the results on expressivity, trainability, noise, and scalability, and applications in high-energy physics, chemistry, finance, healthcare, earth observation, and transportation. An audit of datasets and evaluation practices leads to a minimum reporting protocol. Our main finding is that the design space is rich and symmetry is the best-supported inductive bias, whereas the evidence remains thin: results are mostly simulated, on small graphs, at parity with classical models or with fewer parameters, and no advantage has yet been established on tasks over classical graph data. We identify five open challenges and a research agenda covering provable advantage, equivariant and geometric designs, fault-tolerant implementations, and shared benchmarks. The coverage extends through 2026 and emphasizes recent work.
  </details>

- **[From Local Prediction Error to Final Outputs: A Theory of Expert Sharing in Multi-Step Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17879)**  `arXiv:2611.17879`  
  _Tiantong Wang, Zhiren Gong, Yixin Wang, Tiantong Wu, Wei Yang Bryan Lim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models can share experts across layers or computational steps, pooling training information from different contexts. This can reduce local prediction error, but multi-step systems are ultimately evaluated by their final outputs. We study when these local gains survive composition. In specified linear regression models, we derive exact sharing and merging criteria by balancing variance savings against bias from sharing across roles with different target behavior. Under a common fixed linear propagation map, we characterize expected squared final-output risk, retaining error directions and correlations. We construct cases where sharing lowers average local error yet increases final-output error. Within this setting, we also identify when orthogonal full pooling with isotropic noise preserves every local improvement at the final output. For general routed computations, we derive conditional end-to-end error bounds that account for distribution shifts, errors in surrounding components, and changes in expert selection. Separately, we show that correct predictions and route labels alone do not establish that individual experts implement the attributed operations. Controlled experiments realize a strict local-to-final risk reversal. In low-data symbolic settings, trained Transformers exhibit statistically supported teacher-forced one-step gains without statistically resolved free-running final-output gains. Separate intervention tests distinguish successful multi-layer actions from single-expert attribution and generalization to unseen transitions. Together, these results distinguish when sharing improves local estimation, when those gains survive to the final output, and what evidence is needed to attribute reusable operations to individual experts.
  </details>

- **[Process Mining-Enabled Federated Anomaly Detection for the Industrial IoT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17869)**  `arXiv:2611.17869`  
  _Francesco Vitale, Salvatore Bramante, Francesco Flammini, Hangli Ge_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While federated anomaly detection enables the integration of local data insights across diverse nodes to reduce false positives, it inherently struggles with non-IID data. In practice, industrial IoT plants often operate under varying conditions and experience diverse environmental phenomena, resulting in significant heterogeneity in the monitored time series. In such settings, standard federated learning approaches fail to perform nuanced aggregation that accounts for these cross-node differences, ultimately leading to increased false positive rates. In this paper, we propose a process mining approach to evaluate the similarity of local node data against a reference provided by a global node. The global node utilizes these similarity metrics to dynamically weight local models during aggregation, producing personalized updates distributed across the network. We evaluate our approach using industrial IoT time-series data collected from manufacturing plants operating under distinct conditions. Experimental results demonstrate that our process mining-enabled federated anomaly detection pipeline achieves superior performance, yielding a 46.52% and 11.99% relative improvement in F1-score compared to isolated local training baselines and standard FedAvg, respectively, alongside a 51.24% relative reduction in false positives over standard federated aggregation.
  </details>

- **[Disentangling Forgetting from Utility: Lightweight LLM Unlearning via Orthogonal Null-Space Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17864)**  `arXiv:2611.17864`  
  _Xiuyuan Wang, Weiming Liu, Zongxin Yang, Fan Wang, Liemiao Jin, Rubin Qin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) pose significant security risks by retaining private or hazardous information, creating an urgent need for unlearning techniques that excise specific knowledge without compromising general utility. However, prevailing fine-tuning-based unlearning methods struggle to achieve this balance, often suffering from computational inefficiency and unintended utility degradation due to the entangled nature of knowledge within model parameters. To disentangle forgetting from utility, we propose UUE, a theoretically grounded framework that reformulates unlearning as controllable model editing. First, UUE projects parameter updates into the orthogonal left null space of retained knowledge representations, strictly ensuring that the editing process causes minimal interference to the model's general capabilities. Second, distinct from targeted mapping, we introduce a convex untargeted objective that derives a closed-form analytical solution to effectively disrupt undesired knowledge by pushing forget-set embeddings toward their antipodal points. We implement UUE via lightweight, pluggable adapters (including a low-rank variant, UUE-L), enabling efficient deployment without modifying the frozen backbone. Extensive experiments on TOFU and WMDP benchmarks demonstrate that UUE and UUE-L achieve superior unlearning efficacy, significantly outperforming existing methods.
  </details>

- **[Fuse-All MoE Inference Kernel with Load Balancing on TPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17857)**  `arXiv:2611.17857`  
  _Rupeng Liu, Bangsheng Tang, Zongwei Zhou, Chengji Yao, Yejin Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
